\alpha
and remains the same (
\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
) as in
Theorem

5.1
.
Therefore, we get two cases:

(i)
if
\lambda^{*}(\delta/2)>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
, the top two eigenvalues of
D
are repeated outliers;
otherwise,
(ii)

\overline{\lambda}(\delta)\geq\lambda^{*}(\delta/2)
and there is no outlier eigenvalue in the limiting spectrum of
D
.

Remark 5.3

(Explicit formulas)
.

By the definition of
\zeta(\lambda;\delta)
(cf.
Equation

3.4
), we can write the limits of the eigenvalues in the following more explicit form, which will be convenient in
Section

6
:

\displaystyle\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1});\delta)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})\left(\frac{1}{\delta}+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-Z}\right]\right),&\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)\\
\overline{\lambda}(\delta)\left(\frac{1}{\delta}+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{\overline{\lambda}(\delta)-Z}\right]\right),&\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})\leq\overline{\lambda}(\delta)\end{cases},

\displaystyle\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2});\delta)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})\left(\frac{1}{\delta}+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})-Z}\right]\right),&\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)\\
\overline{\lambda}(\delta)\left(\frac{1}{\delta}+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{\overline{\lambda}(\delta)-Z}\right]\right),&\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})\leq\overline{\lambda}(\delta)\end{cases},

\displaystyle\zeta(\overline{\lambda}(\delta);\delta)

\displaystyle=\overline{\lambda}(\delta)\left(\frac{1}{\delta}+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{\overline{\lambda}(\delta)-Z}\right]\right).

Proof of
Theorem

5.1
.

The proof is divided into three steps.
Specifically, we first write
D
as the sum of two asymptotically free spiked random matrices in
Section

5.1
.
Then, the limit of
\lambda_{3}(D)
is determined in
Lemma

5.2
.
Finally, the limits of
\lambda_{1}(D),\lambda_{2}(D)
and the monotonicity properties of the limiting eigenvalues in
Items

1
,
2
and
3
of the theorem are given by
Lemma

5.3
.
∎

5.1
Reduction to free additive convolution

To begin with, assume for notational convenience that the latent variable
\eta_{i}=1
for
1\leq i\leq n_{1}
and
\eta_{i}=0
for
n_{1}+1\leq i\leq n
, for some
0\leq n_{1}\leq n
.
Let
n_{2}=n-n_{1}
.
We have almost surely
n_{1}/d\to\delta_{1},n_{2}/d\to\delta_{2}
since the mixing variables are independent and Bernoulli-distributed with mean
\alpha
.
We can write the matrices of interest in block form:

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}A_{1}\\
A_{2}\end{bmatrix},\quad T=\begin{bmatrix}T_{1}&0_{n_{1}\times n_{2}}\\
0_{n_{2}\times n_{1}}&T_{2}\end{bmatrix},

(5.1)

where
A_{1}\in{\mathbb R}^{n_{1}\times d},A_{2}\in{\mathbb R}^{n_{2}\times d}
and
T_{1}\in{\mathbb R}^{n_{1}\times n_{1}},T_{2}\in{\mathbb R}^{n_{2}\times n_{2}}
.
We also let
{\underline{\varepsilon}}_{1}=(\varepsilon_{1},\cdots,\varepsilon_{n_{1}})
and
{\underline{\varepsilon}}_{2}=(\varepsilon_{n_{1}+1},\cdots,\varepsilon_{n})
.
Then,

\displaystyle A^{\top}TA

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}A_{1}^{\top}&A_{2}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}T_{1}&0_{n_{1}\times n_{2}}\\
0_{n_{2}\times n_{1}}&T_{2}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}A_{1}\\
A_{2}\end{bmatrix}=A_{1}^{\top}T_{1}A_{1}+A_{2}^{\top}T_{2}A_{2}.

(5.2)

Note that, for
i\in\{1,2\}
,

\displaystyle A_{i}^{\top}T_{i}A_{i}

\displaystyle=A_{i}^{\top}\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{T}}(q(A_{i}x_{i}^{*},{\underline{\varepsilon}}_{i})))A_{i}.

Since
A_{1},x_{1}^{*},{\underline{\varepsilon}}_{1}
and
A_{2},x_{2}^{*},{\underline{\varepsilon}}_{2}
are mutually independent, we know that
A_{1}^{\top}T_{1}A_{1}
is independent of
A_{2}^{\top}T_{2}A_{2}
.
However,
A_{1}
and
T_{1}
are
not
independent, neither are
A_{2}
and
T_{2}
.

Let
O\sim\haar({\mathbb O}(d))
be a matrix sampled uniformly from the orthogonal group
{\mathbb O}(d)
and independent of everything else.
Then, we have

\displaystyle A_{1}^{\top}T_{1}A_{1}+A_{2}^{\top}T_{2}A_{2}

\displaystyle=A_{1}^{\top}\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{T}}(q(A_{1}x_{1}^{*},{\underline{\varepsilon}}_{1})))A_{1}+A_{2}^{\top}\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{T}}(q(A_{2}x_{2}^{*},{\underline{\varepsilon}}_{2})))A_{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}A_{1}^{\top}\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{T}}(q(A_{1}x_{1}^{*},{\underline{\varepsilon}}_{1})))A_{1}+(A_{2}O)^{\top}\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{T}}(q((A_{2}O)x_{2}^{*},{\underline{\varepsilon}}_{2})))(A_{2}O)

(5.3)

\displaystyle=A_{1}^{\top}\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{T}}(q(A_{1}x_{1}^{*},{\underline{\varepsilon}}_{1})))A_{1}+O^{\top}A_{2}^{\top}\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{T}}(q(A_{2}(Ox_{2}^{*}),{\underline{\varepsilon}}_{2})))A_{2}O

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}A_{1}^{\top}\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{T}}(q(A_{1}x_{1}^{*},{\underline{\varepsilon}}_{1})))A_{1}+O^{\top}A_{2}^{\top}\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{T}}(q(A_{2}x_{2}^{*},{\underline{\varepsilon}}_{2})))A_{2}O

(5.4)

\displaystyle=A_{1}^{\top}T_{1}A_{1}+O^{\top}A_{2}^{\top}T_{2}A_{2}O.

Equation

5.3
follows from the independence of
A_{1},A_{2}
, and from the rotational invariance of isotropic Gaussians.

Equation

5.4
follows since
O
and
Ox_{2}^{*}
are independent if
O\sim\haar({\mathbb O}(d))
and
x_{2}^{*}\sim\unif({\mathbb S}^{d-1})
.
In this step, we crucially use the assumption that
x_{1}^{*}
and
x_{2}^{*}
are independent and each uniformly distributed over
{\mathbb S}^{d-1}
.

Now
A_{1}^{\top}T_{1}A_{1}
and
O^{\top}A_{2}^{\top}T_{2}A_{2}O
are
asymptotically free
.
(See
Appendix

F
for a definition of asymptotic freeness and a primer on free probability theory.)
Therefore, we can study
A_{1}^{\top}T_{1}A_{1}
and
A_{2}^{\top}T_{2}A_{2}
separately by using existing results, and then apply tools from free probability theory to characterize their sum.
In particular, to understand
A_{i}^{\top}T_{i}A_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, we use a theorem from
[
MM19
]
(transcribed in
Theorem

I.5
);
to understand
A_{1}^{\top}T_{1}A_{1}+A_{2}^{\top}T_{2}A_{2}
, we use a theorem from
[
BBCF17
]
(transcribed in
Theorem

G.1
).

5.2
Right edge of the bulk of
D

Before proceeding to the analysis, let us introduce some more notation.
Let

\displaystyle D_{1}=\frac{1}{n}A_{1}^{\top}T_{1}A_{1},\quad D_{2}=\frac{1}{n}A_{2}^{\top}T_{2}A_{2}.

(5.5)

Therefore
D=D_{1}+D_{2}
according to
Equation

5.2
.
We first calculate the limiting value of the right edge of the bulk of the spectrum of
D
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Consider the setting of
Section

2.2
and let
Items

(A1)
,
(A2)
,
(A3)
,
(A4)
,
(A5)
and
(A7)
hold.
Denote by
\mu_{D}
the empirical spectral distribution of
D
.
Then

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\sup\supp(\mu_{D})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta}\cdot s^{-1}_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}(-1/\overline{\lambda}(\delta)),

(5.6)

almost surely,
where
\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
is the solution to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z}{\overline{\lambda}(\delta)-Z}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta}

(5.7)

and the function
s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}
is defined as

\displaystyle s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{z}+\delta\,\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{1+zZ}\right].

(5.8)

Remark 5.4
.

The function
s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}
is in fact the inverse
Stieltjes transform
of the free additive convolution of the limiting spectral distributions
\mu_{1}
of
\frac{n}{d}D_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
of
\frac{n}{d}D_{2}
. The function
s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(\lambda)

is precisely
\delta\psi(\lambda;\delta)
, where
\psi(\lambda;\delta)
defined in
Equation

3.2
.
We note also that the parameter
\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
defined in
Equation

5.7
is the same as that defined through
Equation

3.3
.
(See
Lemma

I.2
.)
The connection shall become more transparent in the proof below.

Proof of
Lemma

5.2
.

First note that the scaling factor
\frac{1}{d}
in
Theorem

I.6
(which will be used momentarily) is different from our scaling
\frac{1}{n}
in the definition of
D
(cf.
Equation

2.5
).
We therefore consider
\widetilde{D}=\frac{1}{d}A^{\top}TA
for the convenience of applying
Theorem

I.6
.
All results regarding the matrix
\widetilde{D}
can be translated to
D
by inserting a factor
\frac{d}{n}\to\frac{1}{\delta}
at proper places, since
D=\frac{d}{n}\widetilde{D}
.

Let

\displaystyle\widetilde{D}_{1}=\frac{1}{d}A_{1}^{\top}T_{1}A_{1},\quad\widetilde{D}_{2}=\frac{1}{d}A_{2}^{\top}T_{2}A_{2}.

(5.9)

By
Equation

5.2
,
\widetilde{D}=\widetilde{D}_{1}+\widetilde{D}_{2}
.
Let
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
be the limiting spectral distributions of
\widetilde{D}_{1}
and
\widetilde{D}_{2}
, respectively, as
n_{1},n_{2},d\to\infty
with
n_{1}/d\to\delta_{1}=\alpha\delta
and
n_{2}/d\to\delta_{2}=(1-\alpha)\delta
.
As argued in
Section

5.1
,
\widetilde{D}_{1}
and
\widetilde{D}_{2}
are asymptotically free. Hence,
the limiting spectral distribution of
\widetilde{D}
is given by the free additive convolution of
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
,
denoted by
\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}

[
Voi91
,
Spe93
]
.
It remains to compute
\sup\supp(\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2})
.

A careful inspection of the proof of
[
MM19
, Lemma 2]
shows that the bulk of the spectrum of
\widetilde{D}_{i}
, i.e.,
\lambda_{2}(\widetilde{D}_{i})\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{d}(\widetilde{D}_{i})
, interlaces the spectrum of

E_{i}\coloneqq\frac{1}{d}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{\top}T_{i}\widetilde{A}_{i}\in{\mathbb R}^{(d-1)\times(d-1)}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, respectively.
Specifically,

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(E_{i})\geq\lambda_{2}(\widetilde{D}_{i})\geq\lambda_{2}(E_{i})\geq\lambda_{3}(\widetilde{D}_{i})\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{d-2}(E_{i})\geq\lambda_{d-1}(\widetilde{D}_{i})\geq\lambda_{d-1}(E_{i})\geq\lambda_{d}(\widetilde{D}_{i}).

(5.10)

Here,
T_{i}=\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{T}}(q(A_{i}x_{i}^{*},{\underline{\varepsilon}}_{i})))
(recall
Equation

5.1
) and
\widetilde{A}_{i}\in{\mathbb R}^{n_{i}\times(d-1)}
is an
independent
matrix with i.i.d.
{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
entries.
In particular,
T_{i}
and
\widetilde{A}_{i}
are independent.
For the convenience of applying
Theorem

I.6
, we also define, for
i\in\{1,2\}
,

\widetilde{E}_{i}\coloneqq\frac{1}{d-1}\widetilde{A}_{i}^{\top}T_{i}\widetilde{A}_{i}
. Note that
E_{i}=\frac{d-1}{d}\widetilde{E}_{i}
.
Also
n_{1}/(d-1)\to\delta_{1}
and
n_{2}/(d-1)\to\delta_{2}
.

Since each
y_{i}
(for
1\leq i\leq n
) is i.i.d., the limiting spectral distributions of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are in fact the same and both equal the law of
Z
.
Thus,
Theorem

I.6
provides us with a characterization of the limiting spectral distribution of
\widetilde{E}_{i}
:

\displaystyle\mu_{\widetilde{E}_{1}}

\displaystyle\to\widetilde{\mu}_{1},\quad\mu_{\widetilde{E}_{2}}\to\widetilde{\mu}_{2},

weakly as
n_{1},n_{2},d\to\infty
with
n_{1}/(d-1)\to\delta_{1},n_{2}/(d-1)\to\delta_{2}
.
Furthermore, the limiting spectral distributions admit the following explicit description through the inverse Stieltjes transform (see
Equation

F.1
in
Appendix

F
for the definition of
Stieltjes transform
of a distribution):

\displaystyle s_{\widetilde{\mu}_{1}}^{-1}(z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{z}+\delta_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{1+zZ}\right],\quad s_{\widetilde{\mu}_{2}}^{-1}(z)=-\frac{1}{z}+\delta_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{1+zZ}\right].

(5.11)

In view of the scaling factor
\frac{d-1}{d}\to 1
, the limiting spectral distributions of
E_{1},E_{2}
are also given by
\widetilde{\mu}_{1},\widetilde{\mu}_{2}
, respectively.
Recall that the bulks of the spectra of
\widetilde{D}_{1},\widetilde{D}_{2}
interlace the spectra of
E_{1},E_{2}
, respectively (cf.
Equation

5.10
).
Since
\widetilde{D}_{1}
and
\widetilde{D}_{2}
can each have at most one outlier eigenvalue (cf.
Theorem

I.5
), the limiting spectral distributions
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
of
\widetilde{D}_{1},\widetilde{D}_{2}
, respectively, are the same as
\widetilde{\mu}_{1},\widetilde{\mu}_{2}
whose inverse Stieltjes transforms are shown in
Equation

5.11
.

The following two well-known facts in free probability theory (cf.
Equations

F.2
and
F.3
in
Appendix

F
)

\displaystyle R_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}(z)

\displaystyle=R_{\mu_{1}}(z)+R_{\mu_{2}}(z),

and

\displaystyle R_{\mu}(z)

\displaystyle=s_{\mu}^{-1}(-z)-\frac{1}{z},

then allows us to compute
s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}
.
Indeed

\displaystyle s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(z)

\displaystyle=s_{\mu_{1}}^{-1}(z)+s_{\mu_{2}}^{-1}(z)+\frac{1}{z}=s_{\widetilde{\mu}_{1}}^{-1}(z)+s_{\widetilde{\mu}_{2}}^{-1}(z)+\frac{1}{z}=-\frac{1}{z}+\delta\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{1+zZ}\right].

(5.12)

Given
s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}
, one can calculate
\sup\supp(\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2})
which is in turn the limiting value of
\sup\supp(\mu_{\widetilde{D}})
, where
\mu_{\widetilde{D}}
denotes the empirical spectral distribution of
\widetilde{D}
.
This can be accomplished thanks to the results in
[
BY12
, Lemma 3.1]
(see also
[
SC95
, Sec. 4]
):

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\sup\supp(\mu_{\widetilde{D}})

\displaystyle=\sup\supp(\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2})

(5.13)

\displaystyle=\min_{\lambda>\sup\supp(Z)}s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\lambda)

\displaystyle=\min_{\lambda>\sup\supp(Z)}\lambda+\delta\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{1-Z/\lambda}\right].

(5.14)

The convergence in
Equation

5.13
holds almost surely since

\displaystyle\mu_{\widetilde{D}}

\displaystyle=\mu_{\widetilde{D}_{1}+\widetilde{D}_{2}}\to\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}

weakly
[
Voi91
,
Spe93
]
.
To solve the minimization problem in
Equation

5.14
, we observe that the function
s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\lambda)
can be written in terms of
\psi(\lambda;\delta)
defined in
Equation

3.2
:

\displaystyle s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\lambda)

\displaystyle=\lambda+\delta\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{1-Z/\lambda}\right]=\delta\lambda\left(\frac{1}{\delta}+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{\lambda-Z}\right]\right)=\delta\cdot\psi(\lambda;\delta).

Since
\psi(\lambda;\delta)
is convex in the first argument (cf.
Lemma

I.1
), so is
s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\lambda)
as a function of
\lambda
.
As a result, the minimizer
\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
in
Equation

5.14
is the critical point of
s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\lambda)
.
That is,

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\lambda)\right|_{\lambda=\overline{\lambda}(\delta)}

\displaystyle=1-\delta\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z}{\overline{\lambda}(\delta)-Z}\right)^{2}\right]=0,

i.e.,
\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
is the solution to the following equation

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z}{\overline{\lambda}(\delta)-Z}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta}.

(5.15)

The minimum value in
Equation

5.14
is therefore

\displaystyle s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\overline{\lambda}(\delta))

\displaystyle=\overline{\lambda}(\delta)\left(1+\delta\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{\overline{\lambda}(\delta)-Z}\right]\right).

(5.16)

At this point, we have successfully computed the limiting value of
\sup\supp(\mu_{\widetilde{D}})
.
However, recall that the original matrix we are interested in is
D=\frac{d}{n}(\widetilde{D}_{1}+\widetilde{D}_{2})
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\sup\supp(\mu_{D})

\displaystyle=\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{d}{n}\sup\supp(\mu_{\widetilde{D}})=\overline{\lambda}(\delta)\left(\frac{1}{\delta}+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{\overline{\lambda}(\delta)-Z}\right]\right),

where
\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
satisfies
Equation

5.15
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

5.3
Outlier eigenvalues of
D

Finally, we need to understand the outliers in the spectrum of
D
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Consider the setting of
Section

2.2
and let
Items

(A1)
,
(A2)
,
(A3)
,
(A4)
,
(A5)
and
(A7)
hold.
Let the function
s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\lambda)
be given by
Equation

5.8
.
Then, the following statements hold.

1.

\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})>\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})
;

2.

If
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})>\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
, then
s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1}))>s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2}))
;

3.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, if
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
, then
s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i}))>s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\overline{\lambda}(\delta))
;

4.

We have that, almost surely,

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{1}(D)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta}\cdot s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\max\{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1}),\overline{\lambda}(\delta)\}),

(5.17)

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{2}(D)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta}\cdot s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\max\{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2}),\overline{\lambda}(\delta)\}),

(5.18)

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{3}(D)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta}\cdot\sup\supp(\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2})=\frac{1}{\delta}\cdot s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\overline{\lambda}(\delta)).

(5.19)

Remark 5.5
.

Recalling the definition
\zeta(\lambda;\delta)=\psi(\max\{\lambda,\overline{\lambda}(\delta)\};\delta)
(cf.
Equation

3.4
) and the relation
\frac{1}{\delta}\cdot s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\lambda)=\psi(\lambda;\delta)
(cf.
Remark

5.4
), we can write the limiting values of
\lambda_{1}(D),\lambda_{2}(D),\lambda_{3}(D)
in
Equations

5.17
,
5.18
and
5.19
as

\displaystyle\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1});\delta)\geq\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2});\delta)\geq\zeta(\overline{\lambda}(\delta);\delta),

(5.20)

respectively.
To see why the above chain of inequalities holds, note that by
Item

3
of
Lemma

5.3
,
\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i});\delta)>\zeta(\overline{\lambda}(\delta);\delta)
if
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
and
\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i});\delta)=\zeta(\overline{\lambda}(\delta);\delta)
otherwise.
So

\displaystyle\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i});\delta)\geq\zeta(\overline{\lambda}(\delta);\delta)

(5.21)

is always true for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Also, by
Item

2
of
Lemma

5.3
,
\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1});\delta)\geq\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2});\delta)>\zeta(\overline{\lambda}(\delta);\delta)
if
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})\geq\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
.
If
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})\geq\overline{\lambda}(\delta)\geq\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})
,
\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2});\delta)=\zeta(\overline{\lambda}(\delta);\delta)\leq\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1});\delta)
by
Equation

5.21
.
If
\overline{\lambda}(\delta)\geq\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})\geq\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})
,
\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1});\delta)=\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2});\delta)=\zeta(\overline{\lambda}(\delta);\delta)
.
All cases have been exhausted in light of
Item

1
of
Lemma

5.3
.
In any case,

\displaystyle\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1});\delta)\geq\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2});\delta)

(5.22)

holds.

Equation

5.20
then follows from
Equations

5.21
and
5.22
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.3
.

The proof is divided into three parts.
We first explicitly evaluate the theoretical predictions of the limiting values of the top three eigenvalues of
D
given by
Equation

G.1
of
Theorem

G.1
.
The convergence of the outlier eigenvalues and the right edge of the bulk to the respective predictions is then formally justified in the second part.
Finally, several properties concerning the spectral threshold and the limiting eigenvalues are proved in the third part.

Limiting eigenvalues.

To understand the outlier eigenvalues of
D=D_{1}+D_{2}
, we need to first understand the outlier eigenvalues of
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
individually.
The latter quantities have been characterized by
[
MM19
, Lemma 2]
(transcribed in
Theorem

I.5
).
To calibrate the scaling, let us define

\displaystyle D_{1}^{\prime}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n_{1}}A_{1}^{\top}T_{1}A_{1},\quad D_{2}^{\prime}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n_{2}}A_{2}^{\top}T_{2}A_{2}.

Theorem

I.5
then applies to the above matrices
D_{1}^{\prime},D_{2}^{\prime}
and implies that each of
D_{1}^{\prime}
and
D_{2}^{\prime}
has a potential outlier eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}(D_{1}^{\prime})
and
\lambda_{1}(D_{2}^{\prime})
, respectively.
As
n_{1},n_{2},d\to\infty
with
n_{1}/d\to\delta_{1}
and
n_{2}/d\to\delta_{2}
, they converge almost surely to the following limiting values:

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{1}(D_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1});\delta_{1}),\quad\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{1}(D_{2}^{\prime})=\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2});\delta_{2}),

where
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})
and
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})
are the solutions to

\displaystyle\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1});\delta_{1})

\displaystyle=\varphi(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})),\quad\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2});\delta_{2})=\varphi(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})),

respectively.
For
i\in\{1,2\}
, let us assume that
\lambda_{1}(D_{i}^{\prime})
is indeed an outlier eigenvalue of
D_{i}^{\prime}
, that is, its limiting value
\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i});\delta_{i})
lies outside the bulk of the limiting spectrum of
D_{i}^{\prime}
.
According to
Theorem

I.5
, this happens if and only if
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{i})
.
In this case, the limiting value of the outlier eigenvalue can be written more explicitly as

\displaystyle\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i});\delta_{i})=\psi(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i});\delta_{i})=\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{i}}+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})-Z}\right]\right),

(5.23)

where
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})
is the solution to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z(G^{2}-1)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})-Z}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta_{i}}.

(5.24)

Let us first translate the above result (i.e.,
Equations

5.23
and
5.24
) regarding
D_{1}^{\prime},D_{2}^{\prime}
to
\widetilde{D}_{1},\widetilde{D}_{2}
defined in
Equation

5.9
.
Since
n_{1}/d\to\delta_{1},n_{2}/d\to\delta_{2}
and
\widetilde{D}_{1}=\frac{n_{1}}{d}D_{1}^{\prime},\widetilde{D}_{2}=\frac{n_{2}}{d}D_{2}^{\prime}
, we have that, almost surely,

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{1}(\widetilde{D}_{i})

\displaystyle=\delta_{i}\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{i}}+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})-Z}\right]\right)=\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})\left(1+\delta_{i}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})-Z}\right]\right)\eqqcolon\theta_{i},

where we have denoted the limiting value of
\lambda_{1}(\widetilde{D}_{i})
by
\theta_{i}
.
In view of the definition of
s_{\mu_{i}}^{-1}
in
Equation

5.11
, we recognize that

\displaystyle\theta_{i}=s_{\mu_{i}}^{-1}(-1/\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})).

(5.25)

Provided with the individual outlier of
\widetilde{D}_{i}
(cf.
Equation

5.25
),
we now invoke
[
BBCF17
, Theorem 2.1]
(transcribed in
Theorem

G.1
) to determine how an outlier of
\widetilde{D}_{i}
is mapped to the spectrum of
\widetilde{D}
by the free additive convolution.
According to
Theorem

G.1
, the limiting value, denoted by
\rho_{i}
, of the potential outlier of
\widetilde{D}=\widetilde{D}_{1}+\widetilde{D}_{2}
resulting from
\theta_{i}
is given by

\displaystyle\rho_{i}\coloneqq w_{i}^{-1}(\theta_{i}),

(5.26)

where
w_{1},w_{2}
are the pair of
subordination functions
associated with the pair of distributions
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
.

As the name suggests,
w_{1},w_{2}
enjoy the following subordination property (cf.
[
BBCF17
, Sec. 3.4.1]
or
Equation

F.4
in
Appendix

F
):

\displaystyle s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}(z)

\displaystyle=s_{\mu_{1}}(w_{1}(z))=s_{\mu_{2}}(w_{2}(z))=\frac{1}{z-(w_{1}(z)+w_{2}(z))}.

(5.27)

To understand the value of
\rho_{i}=w_{i}^{-1}(\theta_{i})
(cf.
Equation

5.26
), let us compute

\displaystyle s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}(w_{i}^{-1}(\theta_{i}))

\displaystyle=s_{\mu_{i}}(\theta_{i})=-1/\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i}).

(5.28)

The first equality is by the subordination property (
Equation

5.27
) and the second one by the observation in
Equation

5.25
.

Equation

5.28
then gives

\displaystyle\rho_{i}

\displaystyle=w_{i}^{-1}(\theta_{i})=s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})).

(5.29)

To translate the result in
Equation

5.29
regarding
\widetilde{D}
to
D
defined in
Equation

5.5
, we simply note that
D=\frac{d}{n}\widetilde{D}
and
d/n\to 1/\delta
.
Therefore, the limiting eigenvalue of
D
resulting from the outlier eigenvalue of
D_{i}
is given by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\cdot\rho_{i}=\frac{1}{\delta}\cdot s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})),

(5.30)

almost surely.

Convergence of eigenvalues.

We then formally justify that the right edge of the bulk and the outlier eigenvalues of
D
indeed converge to the theoretical predictions in
Equations

5.6
and
5.30
, respectively, as
d\to\infty
, therefore confirming the validity of the latter formulas.
Let
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}\coloneqq\supp(\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2})
.
For
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
{\mathcal{K}}_{i}
be the singleton set
\{\rho_{i}\}
if
\theta_{i}\notin\supp(\mu_{i})
and
\emptyset
otherwise.
Let
{\mathcal{K}}\coloneqq{\mathcal{K}}_{0}\cup{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
.
Then the first statement of
[
BBCF17
, Theorem 2.1]
(transcribed in
Theorem

G.1
) guarantees that for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\exists d_{0},\,\forall d>d_{0},\,\{\lambda_{i}(\widetilde{D})\}_{i=1}^{d}\subset{\mathcal{K}}_{\varepsilon}\right]

\displaystyle=1,

(5.31)

where
{\mathcal{K}}_{\varepsilon}
denotes the
\varepsilon
-enlargement
of
{\mathcal{K}}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\left\{\rho\in{\mathbb R}:\inf_{\rho^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}}|\rho-\rho^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon\right\}.

In words,
Equation

5.31
says that almost surely for every sufficiently large dimension
d
, the spectrum of
\widetilde{D}
is contained in an arbitrarily small neighbourhood of
{\mathcal{K}}
.
Furthermore, suppose
\rho\in{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
and
\rho\notin{\mathcal{K}}_{0}
, that is,
\rho
is an outlier in the limiting spectrum of
\widetilde{D}
.
Assume also that
\varepsilon>0
is sufficiently small so that
(\rho-2\varepsilon,\rho+2\varepsilon)\cap{\mathcal{K}}=\{\rho\}
.
Then

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\exists d_{0},\,\forall d>d_{0},\,\left|\{\lambda_{i}(\widetilde{D})\}_{i=1}^{d}\cap(\rho-2\varepsilon,\rho+2\varepsilon)\right|=\mathds{1}{\left\{w_{1}(\rho)=\theta_{1}\right\}}+\mathds{1}{\left\{w_{2}(\rho)=\theta_{2}\right\}}\right]

\displaystyle=1.

(5.32)

In words,
Equation

5.32
says that almost surely for every sufficiently large dimension
d
, the outlier
\theta_{1}
(resp.
\theta_{2}
) in the limiting spectrum of
\widetilde{D}_{1}
(resp.
\widetilde{D}_{2}
) is mapped to
w_{1}^{-1}(\theta_{1})
(resp.
w_{2}^{-1}(\theta_{2})
) in the limiting spectrum of
\widetilde{D}
.
Since
D,D_{1},D_{2}
and
\widetilde{D},\widetilde{D}_{1},\widetilde{D}_{2}
only differ by a
\delta
factor, similar statements hold true for
D,D_{1},D_{2}
as well.

Combining
Equations

5.30
,
5.31
and
5.32
yields
Equations

5.17
,
5.18
and
5.19
in
Item

4
of
Lemma

5.3
.

Properties of spectral threshold and limiting eigenvalues.

We identify under what condition
\rho_{i}=w_{i}^{-1}(\theta_{i})
is an outlier in the limiting spectrum of
\widetilde{D}
.
For this to be the case,
\theta_{i}
is necessarily an outlier in the limiting spectrum of
\widetilde{D}_{i}
, which is assumed in the preceding derivations.
As
Theorem

I.5
guaranteed, a sufficient and necessary condition for this event is
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{i})
.
Under the free additive convolution, the outlier
\theta_{i}
of
\widetilde{D}_{i}
is then mapped to
w_{i}^{-1}(\theta_{i})
.
Let us compare
w_{i}^{-1}(\theta_{i})
with
\sup\supp(\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2})
, i.e., the right edge of the bulk of the limiting spectral distribution of
\widetilde{D}=\widetilde{D}_{1}+\widetilde{D}_{2}
.
The former quantity equals
s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i}))
(as derived in
Equation

5.29
) and the latter one equals
s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\overline{\lambda}(\delta))
(see
Equation

5.16
in the proof of
Lemma

5.2
).
Recall the following two facts:

1.

s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\lambda)=\delta\cdot\psi(\lambda;\delta)
(as observed in
Remark

5.4
);

2.

\psi(\lambda;\delta)
is convex in
\lambda
and increasing for
\lambda\in[\overline{\lambda}(\delta),\infty)
(proved in
Lemma

I.1
).

We therefore conclude that
s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i}))>s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\overline{\lambda}(\delta))
if
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
.
This establishes
Item

3
of
Lemma

5.3
.
This condition is more stringent than the previous one
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{i})
.
This can be seen by inspecting the definitions (see, e.g.,
Equation

I.3
in
Lemma

I.2
) of
\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{i})
and
\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z}{\overline{\lambda}(\delta)-Z}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta},\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z}{\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{i})-Z}\right)^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{\delta_{i}},

(5.33)

respectively, and realizing that
\overline{\lambda}(\delta)>\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{i})
since
\delta>\delta_{i}
.

We pause and make the following remark regarding the effect of the free additive convolution on the outliers in the spectra of the addends.
Comparing
Equation

5.29
with the limiting value of the right edge of the bulk (cf.
Equation

5.16
), we note the following:
\lambda_{1}(\widetilde{D}_{i})
being an outlier eigenvalue of
\widetilde{D}_{i}
does
not
imply that its image
\rho_{i}
under the free additive convolution is also an outlier eigenvalue of
\widetilde{D}=\widetilde{D}_{1}+\widetilde{D}_{2}
.
In fact, it can be buried strictly inside the bulk, which happens if
\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{i})<\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})<\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
.

We then show
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})>\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})
in
Item

1
of
Lemma

5.3
.
Recall that
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})
and
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})
are the unique solutions to
\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1});\delta_{1})=\varphi(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1}))
and
\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2});\delta_{2})=\varphi(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2}))
, respectively.
Since
\zeta(\cdot;\delta_{1}),\zeta(\cdot;\delta_{2})
are non-decreasing and
\varphi(\cdot)
is strictly decreasing, it suffices to show

\displaystyle\zeta(\lambda;\delta_{1})<\zeta(\lambda;\delta_{2})

(5.34)

for any
\lambda>\sup\supp(Z)
.
We do so in four steps.
(The following arguments are best understood with
Figure

2
in mind.)

1.

First we claim that
\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{1})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{2})
.
This follows from a similar observation as in
Equation

5.33
and the assumption
\alpha>1/2
(cf.
Item

(A2)
) which implies
\delta_{1}>\delta_{2}
.

2.

Second we claim that
\psi(\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{1});\delta_{1})<\psi(\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{2});\delta_{2})
.
Indeed,

\displaystyle\psi(\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{1});\delta_{1})

\displaystyle<\psi(\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{2});\delta_{1})<\psi(\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{2});\delta_{2}).

The first inequality follows since
\psi(\lambda;\delta_{1})
is strictly decreasing for
\lambda\leq\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{1})
(see
Item

2
of
Lemma

I.1
) and
\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{1})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{2})
as shown in
Item

1
above.
The second inequality follows since

\displaystyle\psi(\cdot;\delta_{1})<\psi(\cdot;\delta_{2})

(5.35)

for any
\lambda>\sup\supp(Z)
(see the definition of
\psi
in
Equation

3.2
and also
Item

3
of
Lemma

I.1
).
Note that in this step we use
\sup\supp(Z)>0
in
Item

(A7)
.
This shows that
Equation

5.34
holds for any
\lambda\leq\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{2})
.

3.

We then claim that
Equation

5.34
holds for any
\lambda\geq\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{1})
.
This is because, in this regime, we have

\displaystyle\zeta(\lambda;\delta_{1})=\psi(\lambda;\delta_{1})<\psi(\lambda;\delta_{2})=\zeta(\lambda;\delta_{2})

using the definition of
\zeta(\cdot;\delta_{i})
(cf.
Equation

3.4
) and
Equation

5.35
.

4.

Finally, it remains to verify that
Equation

5.34
holds for
\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{2})\leq\lambda\leq\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{1})
.
Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\zeta(\lambda;\delta_{1})

\displaystyle=\psi(\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{1});\delta_{1})<\psi(\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{2});\delta_{2})<\psi(\lambda;\delta_{2}).

The equality is by definition of
\zeta(\cdot;\delta_{1})
.
The first inequality is by
Item

2
above.
The second inequality follows since
\psi(\cdot;\delta_{2})
is strictly increasing for
\lambda\geq\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{2})
(see
Item

2
of
Lemma

I.1
).

Combining
Items

1
,
2
,
3
and
4
above then proves
Equation

5.34
which implies
Item

1
of
Lemma

5.3
.

Since
\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
is the (unique) critical point of
s_{\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}}^{-1}(-1/\lambda)
which is increasing for
\lambda\geq\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
,
Item

2
of
Lemma

5.3
then follows.

The proof of the whole lemma is complete.
∎

6
Joint distribution via Approximate Message Passing

The limiting joint distribution in
Theorem

3.1
is obtained via a
generalized approximate message passing
(GAMP) algorithm whose iterates converge to the top two eigenvectors of
D=A^{\top}TA
.
Within this section, we adopt the following rescaling for the convenience of applying the GAMP machinery:

\displaystyle\overline{A}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\,A,\quad\overline{x}_{1}^{*}\coloneqq\sqrt{d}\,x_{1}^{*},\quad\overline{x}_{2}^{*}\coloneqq\sqrt{d}\,x_{2}^{*},\quad\overline{D}\coloneqq\overline{A}^{\top}\,T\,\overline{A}=\frac{n}{d}A^{\top}TA.

(6.1)

According to
Items

(A1)
and
(A4)
, we have
\overline{A}_{i,j}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}{\mathcal{N}}(0,1/d)
and
\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\unif(\sqrt{d}\,{\mathbb S}^{d-1})
.
Let
\overline{a}_{i}^{\top}\in{\mathbb R}^{d}
denote the
i
-th row of
\overline{A}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle y_{i}

\displaystyle=q\left(\left\langle a_{i},\eta_{i}x_{1}^{*}+(1-\eta_{i})x_{2}^{*}\right\rangle,\varepsilon_{i}\right)=q\left(\left\langle\overline{a}_{i},\eta_{i}\overline{x}_{1}^{*}+(1-\eta_{i})\overline{x}_{2}^{*}\right\rangle,\varepsilon_{i}\right).

Therefore,
y\in{\mathbb R}^{n}
and related quantities such as
T\in{\mathbb R}^{n\times n}
(defined in
Equation

2.5
) do not have to be rescaled.
The overlaps are invariant under rescaling of
D
.
Furthermore, since
n/d\to\delta
, the limiting eigenvalues of
\overline{D}
are equal to those of
D
multiplied by
\delta
in view of
Equation

6.1
.

We first extend the GAMP algorithm for the non-mixed GLM
[
Ran11
]
and its associated state evolution analysis to the mixed GLM model. The GAMP algorithm is defined in terms of a sequence of Lipschitz functions
g_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb R
and
f_{t+1}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb R
, for
t\geq 0
. For
t\geq 0
, the algorithm iteratively computes
u^{t},{\tilde{u}}^{t}\in{\mathbb R}^{n}
and
v^{t+1},{\tilde{v}}^{t+1}\in{\mathbb R}^{d}
as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}u^{t}&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}\overline{A}{\tilde{v}}^{t}-{\sf b}_{t}{\tilde{u}}^{t-1},\quad{\tilde{u}}^{t}=g_{t}(u^{t};y),\\
v^{t+1}&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}\overline{A}^{\top}{\tilde{u}}^{t}-{\sf c}_{t}{\tilde{v}}^{t},\quad{\tilde{v}}^{t+1}=f_{t+1}(v^{t+1};\,\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*}).\end{split}

(6.2)

The iteration is initialized with a given
{\tilde{v}}^{0}\in{\mathbb R}^{d}
and
{\tilde{u}}^{-1}=0_{n}
. The functions
f_{t}
and
g_{t}
are applied component-wise, i.e.,
f_{t}(v^{t};\,\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*})=(f_{t}(v^{t}_{1};\,\overline{x}_{1,1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2,1}^{*})
,
\ldots,f_{t}(v^{t}_{d};\,\overline{x}_{1,d}^{*},\overline{x}_{2,d}^{*}))
and
g_{t}(u^{t};y)=(g_{t}(u^{t}_{1};y_{1}),\ldots,g_{t}(u^{t}_{n};y_{n}))
. The scalars
{\sf b}_{t},{\sf c}_{t}
are defined as

{\sf b}_{t}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{d}f_{t}^{\prime}(v_{i}^{t};\,\overline{x}_{1,i}^{*},\overline{x}_{2,i}^{*}),\qquad{\sf c}_{t}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{t}^{\prime}(u_{i}^{t};y_{i}),

(6.3)

where
f_{t}^{\prime}
and
g_{t}^{\prime}
each denote the derivative with respect to the first argument.

An important feature of the GAMP algorithm is that as
d\to\infty
, the empirical distributions of the iterates
u^{t}
and
v^{t+1}
converge to the laws of well-defined scalar random variables
U_{t}
and
V_{t+1}
, respectively. Specifically, for
t\geq 0
, let

U_{t}\coloneqq\mu_{1,t}G_{1}+\mu_{2,t}G_{2}+W_{U,t},\quad V_{t+1}\coloneqq\chi_{1,t+1}X_{1}+\chi_{2,t+1}X_{2}+W_{V,t+1},

(6.4)

where
(G_{1},G_{2},W_{U,t})\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0,\sigma_{U,t}^{2})
, and

(X_{1},X_{2},W_{V,t+1})\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0,\sigma_{V,t+1}^{2})
. The random variables
X_{1},X_{2}
are distributed according to limiting laws of the signals
\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*}
, and
G_{1},G_{2}
according to the limiting laws of
\overline{A}\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{A}\overline{x}_{2}^{*}
. Since
\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*}
are independent and uniformly distributed on the sphere, we have

X_{1},X_{2}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
.
The deterministic coefficients

(\mu_{1,t},\mu_{2,t},\sigma_{U,t},\chi_{1,t+1},\chi_{2,t+1},\sigma_{V,t+1})
are computed using the following state evolution recursion:

\displaystyle\mu_{1,t}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}{\mathbb E}[X_{1}f_{t}(V_{t};\,X_{1},X_{2})],\quad\mu_{2,t}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}{\mathbb E}[X_{2}f_{t}(V_{t};\,X_{1},X_{2})],

(6.5)

\displaystyle\sigma_{U,t}^{2}=\frac{1}{\delta}{\mathbb E}[f_{t}(V_{t};\,X_{1},X_{2})^{2}]-\mu_{1,t}^{2}-\mu_{2,t}^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\chi_{1,t+1}=\sqrt{\delta}\left({\mathbb E}[G_{1}g_{t}(U_{t};{\tilde{Y}})]-{\mathbb E}[g_{t}^{\prime}(U_{t};{\tilde{Y}})]\mu_{1,t}\right),\quad\chi_{2,t+1}=\sqrt{\delta}\left({\mathbb E}[G_{2}g_{t}(U_{t};{\tilde{Y}})]-{\mathbb E}[g_{t}^{\prime}(U_{t};{\tilde{Y}})]\mu_{2,t}\right),

\displaystyle\sigma_{V,t+1}^{2}={\mathbb E}[g_{t}(U_{t};{\tilde{Y}})^{2}].

Here the random variable
{\tilde{Y}}
is given by

{\tilde{Y}}=q(\eta G_{1}+(1-\eta)G_{2},\,\varepsilon),\text{ where }(G_{1},G_{2},\eta,\varepsilon)\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\otimes\text{Bern}(\alpha)\otimes P_{\varepsilon}.

(6.6)

The state evolution recursion is initialized in terms of the limiting correlation of the initializer
{\tilde{v}}^{0}
with each of the signals
\overline{x}_{1}^{*}
and
\overline{x}_{2}^{*}
. The existence of these limiting correlations is guaranteed by imposing the following condition on
{\tilde{v}}^{0}
:

(A8)

The initializer
{\tilde{v}}^{0}\in{\mathbb R}^{d}
is independent of
\overline{A}
. Furthermore, there exists a Lipschitz
F_{0}:{\mathbb R}^{2}\to{\mathbb R}
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\langle{\tilde{v}}^{0},\,\Phi(\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*})\rangle}{d}={\mathbb E}[F_{0}(X_{1},X_{2})\Phi(X_{1},X_{2})]\ \text{ almost surely},

(6.7)

for any Lipschitz
\Phi:{\mathbb R}^{2}\to{\mathbb R}
.
Here
X_{1},X_{2}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
.

This assumption is typical in AMP algorithms
[
FVRS22
]
, and our initializer for proving
Theorem

3.1
will be
{\tilde{v}}^{0}=0_{d}
, which trivially satisfies
Item

(A8)
.

Item

(A8)
allows us to initialize the state evolution recursion as:

\displaystyle\mu_{1,0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\langle\overline{x}_{1}^{*}\,,\,{\tilde{v}}^{0}\rangle}{d}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}{\mathbb E}[F_{0}(X_{1},X_{2})X_{1}],\ \ \mu_{2,0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\langle\overline{x}_{2}^{*}\,,\,{\tilde{v}}^{0}\rangle}{d}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}{\mathbb E}[F_{0}(X_{1},X_{2})X_{2}],

\displaystyle\sigma_{U,0}^{2}=\frac{1}{\delta}\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\left\|{\tilde{v}}^{0}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{d}-\mu_{1,0}^{2}-\mu_{2,0}^{2}=\frac{1}{\delta}{\mathbb E}[F_{0}(X_{1},X_{2})^{2}]-\mu_{1,0}^{2}-\mu_{2,0}^{2}.

(6.8)

The sequences of random variables
(W_{U,t})_{t\geq 0}
and

(W_{V,t+1})_{t\geq 0}
in
Equation

6.4
are each jointly Gaussian with zero mean and the following covariance structure:

{\mathbb E}[W_{U,0}W_{U,t}]=\frac{1}{\delta}{\mathbb E}[F_{0}(X_{1},X_{2})\,f_{t}(V_{t};X_{1},X_{2})]-\mu_{1,0}\mu_{1,t}-\mu_{1,0}\mu_{2,t},\qquad t\geq 1,

(6.9)

and for
r,t\geq 1
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb E}[W_{V,r}W_{V,t}]={\mathbb E}\left[g_{r-1}(U_{r-1};{\tilde{Y}})\,g_{t-1}(U_{t-1};{\tilde{Y}})\right],

(6.10)

\displaystyle{\mathbb E}[W_{U,r}W_{U,t}]=\frac{1}{\delta}{\mathbb E}[f_{r}(V_{r};\,X_{1},X_{2})f_{t}(V_{t};\,X_{1},X_{2})]-\mu_{1,r}\mu_{1,t}-\mu_{1,r}\mu_{2,t}.

(6.11)

Note that for
r=t
we have
{\mathbb E}[W_{U,t}^{2}]=\sigma_{U,t}^{2}
and
{\mathbb E}[W_{V,t+1}^{2}]=\sigma_{V,t}^{2}
.

The state evolution result for the GAMP is stated in terms of pseudo-Lipschitz test functions (see
Equation

3.6
).

Proposition 6.1

(State evolution)
.

Consider the setup of
Theorem

3.1
and the GAMP iteration in
Equation

6.2
,
with initialization
{\tilde{v}}^{0}
that satisfies
Item

(A8)
. Assume that for
t\geq 0
, the functions
g_{t}:{\mathbb R}^{2}\to{\mathbb R}
and
f_{t+1}:{\mathbb R}^{3}\to{\mathbb R}
are Lipschitz. Let
g_{1}\coloneqq\overline{A}\overline{x}_{1}^{*},g_{2}\coloneqq\overline{A}\overline{x}_{2}^{*}
.
Then, the following holds almost surely for any
{\rm PL}(2)
function
\Psi:{\mathbb R}^{t+3}\to{\mathbb R}
, for
t\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Psi(g_{1,i},g_{2,i},u^{t}_{i},u^{t-1}_{i},\ldots,u^{0}_{i})={\mathbb E}[\Psi(G_{1},G_{2},\,U_{t},\,U_{t-1},\,\ldots,U_{0})],

(6.12)

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Psi(\overline{x}_{1,i}^{*},\overline{x}_{2,i}^{*},v^{t+1}_{i},v^{t}_{i},\ldots,v^{1}_{i})={\mathbb E}[\Psi(X_{1},\,X_{2},\,V_{t+1},\,V_{t},\,\ldots,V_{1})],

(6.13)

where the distributions of the random vectors
(G_{1},G_{2},U_{t},\ldots,U_{0})
and
(X_{1},X_{2},V_{t+1},\ldots,V_{1})
are given by the state evolution recursion in
Equation

6.4
to (
6.11
).

The proof of the proposition, given in
Appendix

H
, uses a reduction to an abstract AMP recursion with matrix-valued iterates for which a state evolution result was established in
[
JM13
]
.

The result in
Equation

6.13
is equivalent to the statement that the joint empirical distribution of the rows of
(\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*},v^{t},\ldots,v^{1})
converges in Wasserstein-
2
distance to the joint law of

(X_{1},X_{2},V_{t},\ldots,V_{1})
(see
[
FVRS22
, Corollary 7.21]
). A similar equivalence holds for the result in
Equation

6.12
.

Remark 6.1
.

The result in
Proposition

6.1
also applies to the GAMP algorithm in which the memory coefficients
({\sf b}_{t},{\sf c}_{t})
in
Equation

6.3
are replaced with their deterministic limits
\bar{{\sf b}}_{t},\bar{{\sf c}}_{t}
computed via state evolution:

\bar{{\sf b}}_{t}=\frac{1}{\delta}{\mathbb E}[f_{t}^{\prime}(V_{t};\,X_{1},X_{2})],\qquad\bar{{\sf c}}_{t}={\mathbb E}[g_{t}^{\prime}(U_{t};{\tilde{Y}})].

(6.14)

This equivalence follows from an argument similar to
[
FVRS22
, Remark 4.3]
.

6.1
GAMP as a method to compute the linear and spectral estimators

Consider the GAMP iteration in
Equation

6.2
with the initializer
{\tilde{v}}^{0}=0
, and the following choice of functions:

\begin{split}&g_{0}(u^{0};y)=\sqrt{\delta}{\mathcal{L}}(y),\qquad f_{1}(v;\,\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*})=f(\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*}),\\
&g_{t}(u;\,y)=\sqrt{\delta}\,u\,{\mathcal{F}}(y),\quad f_{t+1}(v;\,\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*})=\frac{v}{\beta_{t+1}},\quad t\geq 1,\end{split}

(6.15)

where
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}
is bounded and Lipschitz,
f:{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}
is Lipschitz, and
\beta_{t+1}
is a constant, defined iteratively for
t\geq 0
via the state evolution equations below (
Equation

6.20
). To prove
Theorem

3.1
, we will consider two different choices for the pair of functions
(f,{\mathcal{F}})
, in terms of the spectral preprocessing function
{\mathcal{T}}
(see
Equations

6.24
and
6.25
).

With the above
choice of
f_{t},g_{t}
, the memory coefficients in
Equation

6.3
are given by

{\sf c}_{0}={\sf b}_{1}=0,\qquad{\sf c}_{t}=\sqrt{\delta}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{F}}(y_{i}),\qquad{\sf b}_{t+1}=\frac{1}{\delta\beta_{t+1}}.

(6.16)

Replacing the parameter
{\sf c}_{t}
with its almost sure limit
\bar{{\sf c}}_{t}=\sqrt{\delta}\,{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}({\tilde{Y}})]
, the GAMP iteration becomes

\begin{split}&u^{0}=0,\quad v^{1}=\overline{A}^{\top}{\mathcal{L}}(y),\\
&u^{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}\overline{A}f(\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*}),\quad v^{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}\overline{A}^{\top}Fu^{1}-\sqrt{\delta}{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}({\tilde{Y}})]f(\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*}),\\
&u^{t}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}\,\beta_{t}}\left(\overline{A}v^{t}\,-\,Fu^{t-1}\right),\quad v^{t+1}=\overline{A}^{\top}Fu^{t}-\frac{\sqrt{\delta}}{\beta_{t}}\,{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}({\tilde{Y}})]\,v^{t},\qquad t\geq 2,\end{split}

(6.17)

where
F=\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{F}}(y_{1}),\ldots,{\mathcal{F}}(y_{n}))
. With
f_{t},g_{t}
given by
Equation

6.15
, the initialization for the state evolution in
Equation

6.5
to (
6.8
) is:

\displaystyle\mu_{1,0}=\mu_{2,0}=\sigma_{U,0}^{2}=0,

\displaystyle\chi_{1,1}=\delta{\mathbb E}[G_{1}{\mathcal{L}}({\tilde{Y}})],\quad\chi_{2,1}=\delta{\mathbb E}[G_{2}{\mathcal{L}}({\tilde{Y}})],\quad\sigma_{V,1}^{2}=\delta{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{L}}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}],

\displaystyle\mu_{1,1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}f(X_{1},X_{2})\right],\quad\mu_{2,1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{2}f(X_{1},X_{2})\right],

\displaystyle\sigma_{U,1}^{2}=\frac{1}{\delta}\mathbb{E}\left[f(X_{1},X_{2})^{2}\right]-\mu_{1,1}^{2}-\mu_{2,1}^{2},

(6.18)

where the joint distribution of
(G_{1},G_{2},{\tilde{Y}})
is given by
Equation

6.6
. Furthermore, for
t\geq 1
:

\displaystyle\chi_{1,t+1}=\delta\mu_{1,t}\,{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}({\tilde{Y}})(G_{1}^{2}-1)],\qquad\chi_{2,t+1}=\delta\mu_{2,t}\,{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}({\tilde{Y}})(G_{2}^{2}-1)],

\displaystyle\sigma_{V,t+1}^{2}=\delta\left(\mu_{1,t}^{2}{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}G_{1}^{2}]+\mu_{2,t}^{2}{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}G_{2}^{2}]+\sigma_{U,t}^{2}{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}]\right),

(6.19)

\displaystyle\beta_{t+1}\coloneqq\sqrt{\chi_{1,t+1}^{2}+\chi_{2,t+1}^{2}+\sigma_{V,t+1}^{2}}\,,

(6.20)

\displaystyle\mu_{1,t+1}=\frac{\chi_{1,t+1}}{\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t+1}},\qquad\mu_{2,{t+1}}=\frac{\chi_{2,t+1}}{\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t+1}},\qquad\sigma_{U,t+1}^{2}=\frac{\sigma_{V,t+1}^{2}}{\delta\beta_{t+1}^{2}}.

(6.21)

First note that the iterate
v^{1}
coincides with the linear estimator
\widehat{x}^{\mathrm{lin}}
in
Equation

2.3
. We will show that in the high-dimensional limit the iterate
v^{t}
is aligned with an eigenvector of the matrix
M\coloneqq\overline{A}^{\top}F(\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{\infty}I_{n}+F)^{-1}\overline{A}
, as
t\to\infty
. (
Equation

6.33
shows that
\beta_{\infty}=\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\beta_{t}
is well-defined for our choices of
{\mathcal{F}}
and initializations.) For a heuristic justification of this claim, assume the iterates
u^{t},v^{t}
converge to the limits
u^{\infty},v^{\infty}
in the sense that

\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\lim\limits_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\left\|u^{t}-u^{\infty}\right\|_{2}^{2}=0
and
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\lim\limits_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\left\|v^{t}-v^{\infty}\right\|_{2}^{2}=0
. Then, from
Equation

6.17
these limits satisfy

u^{\infty}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}\,\beta_{\infty}}\left(\overline{A}v^{\infty}\,-\,Fu^{\infty}\right),\qquad v^{\infty}=\overline{A}^{\top}Fu^{\infty}-\frac{\sqrt{\delta}}{\beta_{\infty}}\,{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}({\tilde{Y}})]\,v^{\infty},

(6.22)

which after simplification, can be written as:

v^{\infty}\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{\delta}}{\beta_{\infty}}{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}({\tilde{Y}})]\right)=\overline{A}^{\top}F(\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{\infty}I_{n}+F)^{-1}\overline{A}v^{\infty}.

(6.23)

Therefore,
v^{\infty}
is an eigenvector of the matrix

\overline{A}^{\top}F(\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{\infty}I_{n}+F)^{-1}\overline{A}
, and the GAMP iteration
Equation

6.17
is effectively a power method.

We wish to obtain via GAMP the two leading eigenvectors of the matrix
\overline{A}^{\top}T\overline{A}
, so the heuristic above indicates that we should choose
{\mathcal{F}}
so that
F(\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{\infty}I_{n}+F)^{-1}=c\,T
, for some constant
c
. To this end, we analyze the iteration in
Equation

6.17
with two choices for the function
{\mathcal{F}}(y)
and initialization
{\tilde{v}}^{0}
:

\displaystyle\text{ Choice 1}:\quad{\mathcal{F}}_{1}(y)\coloneqq\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}(y)},\quad f(\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*})=\overline{x}_{1}^{*},

(6.24)

\displaystyle\text{ Choice 2}:\quad{\mathcal{F}}_{2}(y)\coloneqq\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})-{\mathcal{T}}(y)},\quad f(\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*})=\overline{x}_{2}^{*}.

(6.25)

Here, we recall that, for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})
is the unique solution of
\zeta(\lambda;\delta_{i})=\varphi(\lambda)
(see p.
3
).
The initializations in
Equations

6.24
and
6.25
are not feasible in practice since they depend on the unknown signals
\overline{x}_{1}^{*}
and
\overline{x}_{2}^{*}
, but this is not an issue as we use the GAMP in
Equation

6.15
only as a proof technique.

We now examine the state evolution recursion in
Equations

6.19
,
6.20
and
6.21
under each of these choices.

Choice 1.

From
Equation

6.18
, this corresponds to the initialization

\displaystyle\chi_{1,1}=\delta{\mathbb E}[G_{1}{\mathcal{L}}({\tilde{Y}})],\ \chi_{2,1}=\delta{\mathbb E}[G_{2}{\mathcal{L}}({\tilde{Y}})],\ \sigma_{V,1}^{2}=\delta{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{L}}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}],\ \mu_{1,1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}},\ \mu_{2,1}=\sigma_{U,1}^{2}=0.

(6.26)

For
t\geq 1
, the state evolution equations in
Equations

6.19
,
6.20
and
6.21
reduce to:

\begin{split}&\chi_{1,t+1}=\delta\mu_{1,t}\,{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{1}({\tilde{Y}})(G_{1}^{2}-1)],\quad\sigma_{V,t+1}^{2}=\delta\left(\mu_{1,t}^{2}{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{1}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}G_{1}^{2}]\,+\,\sigma_{U,t}^{2}{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{1}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}]\right),\\
&\beta_{t+1}=\sqrt{\chi_{1,t+1}^{2}+\sigma_{V,t+1}^{2}}\,,\qquad\mu_{1,t+1}=\frac{\chi_{1,t+1}}{\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t+1}}\,,\qquad\sigma_{U,t+1}^{2}=\frac{\sigma_{V,t+1}^{2}}{\delta\beta_{t+1}^{2}}\,,\\
\end{split}

(6.27)

and
\mu_{2,t+1}=\chi_{2,t+1}=0
for
t\geq 1
.
Using this in
Proposition

6.1
, we obtain that:

\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{{\langle}\overline{x}_{1}^{*},v^{1}{\rangle}}{d}=\chi_{1,1},\;\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{{\langle}\overline{x}_{2}^{*},v^{1}{\rangle}}{d}=\chi_{2,1},\;\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{{\langle}\overline{x}_{1}^{*},v^{t+1}{\rangle}}{d}=\chi_{1,t+1},\;\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{{\langle}\overline{x}_{2}^{*},v^{t+1}{\rangle}}{d}=0,

(6.28)

for
t\geq 1
. Thus, when initialized with
f(\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*})=\overline{x}_{1}^{*}
, the GAMP iterates
\{v^{t+1}\}_{t\geq 1}
are asymptotically uncorrelated with the signal
\overline{x}_{2}^{*}
.

Choice 2.

This corresponds to the initialization

\displaystyle\chi_{1,1}=\delta{\mathbb E}[G_{1}{\mathcal{L}}({\tilde{Y}})],\ \chi_{2,1}=\delta{\mathbb E}[G_{2}{\mathcal{L}}({\tilde{Y}})],\ \sigma_{V,1}^{2}=\delta{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{L}}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}],\ \mu_{2,1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}},\ \mu_{1,1}=\sigma_{U,1}^{2}=0.

(6.29)

The state evolution equations are:
\mu_{1,t+1}=\chi_{1,t+1}=0
for
t\geq 1
, and

\begin{split}&\chi_{2,t+1}=\delta\mu_{2,t}\,{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{2}({\tilde{Y}})(G_{2}^{2}-1)],\quad\sigma_{V,t+1}^{2}=\delta\left(\mu_{2,t}^{2}{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{2}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}G_{2}^{2}]\,+\,\sigma_{U,t}^{2}{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{2}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}]\right),\\
&\beta_{t+1}=\sqrt{\chi_{2,t+1}^{2}+\sigma_{V,t+1}^{2}}\,,\qquad\mu_{2,t+1}=\frac{\chi_{2,t+1}}{\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t+1}},\qquad\sigma_{U,t+1}^{2}=\frac{\sigma_{V,t+1}^{2}}{\delta\beta_{t+1}^{2}}\,.\end{split}

(6.30)

Using this in
Proposition

6.1
, we obtain that for
t\geq 1
,

\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{{\langle}\overline{x}_{1}^{*},v^{1}{\rangle}}{d}=\chi_{1,1},\;\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{{\langle}\overline{x}_{2}^{*},v^{1}{\rangle}}{d}=\chi_{2,1},\;\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{{\langle}\overline{x}_{1}^{*},v^{t+1}{\rangle}}{d}=0,\;\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{{\langle}\overline{x}_{2}^{*},v^{t+1}{\rangle}}{d}=\chi_{2,t+1}.

(6.31)

The following lemma gives the fixed point of state evolution under choices 1 and 2.

Lemma 6.2

(Limiting values of state evolution parameters)
.

Consider the state evolution recursion under choice
i\in\{1,2\}
. Assume assume that
{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{i}({\tilde{Y}})(G_{i}^{2}-1)]>0
and
\delta>\frac{{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{i}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}]}{({\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{i}({\tilde{Y}})(G_{i}^{2}-1)])^{2}}
. Then, as
t\to\infty
the state evolution parameters
(\chi_{i,t},\sigma_{V,t}^{2})
converge to the fixed point
(\tilde{\chi}_{i},\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{2})
, where

\tilde{\chi}_{i}=\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{\beta}_{i}^{2}(\tilde{\beta}_{i}^{2}-{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{i}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}])}{\tilde{\beta}_{i}^{2}+{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{i}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}G_{i}^{2}]-{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{i}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}]}},\qquad\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{2}=\frac{\tilde{\beta}_{i}^{2}{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{i}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}G_{i}^{2}]}{\tilde{\beta}_{i}^{2}+{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{i}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}G_{i}^{2}]-{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{i}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}]},

(6.32)

and

\tilde{\beta}_{i}^{2}=\tilde{\chi}_{i}^{2}+\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{2}=\delta\,({\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{i}({\tilde{Y}})(G_{i}^{2}-1)])^{2}.

(6.33)

Proof.

The proof is identical to that of Lemma 5.2 in
[
MTV22
]
, which analyzes GAMP for a
non-mixed
GLM with
f_{t},g_{t}
given by
Equation

6.15
. The state evolution recursion under choice 1 in
Equations

6.26
and
6.27
has the same form for all values of
\alpha\in[1/2,1)
. The value of
\alpha
affects the recursion only through the joint distribution of

(\widetilde{Y},G_{1})=(q(\eta G_{1}+(1-\eta)G_{2},\varepsilon),G_{1})
, where
\eta\sim\bern(\alpha)
. The proof of Lemma 5.2 in
[
MTV22
]
does not depend on this joint distribution and applies for any
\alpha
such that the lower bound on
\delta
in the statement of the first part is satisfied. The argument for choice 2, where the joint distribution determining the state evolution in
Equations

6.29
and
6.30
is
(\widetilde{Y},G_{2})=(q(\eta G_{1}+(1-\eta)G_{2},\varepsilon),G_{2})
, is identical. ∎

It is convenient to express the state evolution fixed points in
Equation

6.33
in terms of the joint law of
(G,Y)
, where
Y=q(G,\varepsilon)
, with
G\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
and
\varepsilon\sim P_{\varepsilon}
are independent. Recalling the joint law of
({\tilde{Y}},G_{1},G_{2})
given in
Equation

6.6
and the definitions of
{\mathcal{F}}_{1},{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
in
Equations

6.24
and
6.25
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{1}({\tilde{Y}})]

\displaystyle={\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{1}(Y)]={\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right],\quad{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{1}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}]={\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{1}(Y)^{2}]={\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)^{2}}{(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y))^{2}}\right],

\displaystyle{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{1}({\tilde{Y}})G_{1}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\alpha{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{1}(q(G_{1},\varepsilon))G_{1}^{2}]+(1-\alpha){\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{1}(q(G_{2},\varepsilon))G_{1}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\alpha{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)G^{2}}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right]+(1-\alpha){\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta}+{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right],

(6.34)

where the last equality holds because
{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)(G^{2}-1)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right]=\frac{1}{\delta_{1}}
from
Equation

I.4
, and
\delta_{1}=\alpha\delta
.
Similarly, we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{2}({\tilde{Y}})]={\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{2}(Y)]={\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right],\quad{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{2}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}]={\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)^{2}}{(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y))^{2}}\right],

\displaystyle{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{2}({\tilde{Y}})G_{2}^{2}]=\frac{1}{\delta}+{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right].

(6.35)

Using
Equations

6.34
and
6.35
, the formula for
{\tilde{\beta}}_{i}^{2}
in
Equation

6.33
becomes:

\displaystyle{\tilde{\beta}}_{i}^{2}=\frac{1}{\delta},\qquad i\in\{1,2\}.

(6.36)

We similarly obtain

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}_{i}=\frac{\rho_{i}^{\mathrm{spec}}}{\sqrt{\delta}},\quad\tilde{\sigma}_{1}^{2}=\frac{1-(\rho_{i}^{\mathrm{spec}})^{2}}{\delta},\qquad i\in\{1,2\}.

(6.37)

where
\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}},\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{spec}}
are defined in
Equation

3.5
.

Proof heuristic.

Let us revisit the heuristic sanity-check in
Equation

6.23
. For
i\in\{1,2\}
, under choice
i
with
{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{F}}_{i}
,
F=F_{i}\coloneqq\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{F}}_{i}(y_{1}),\ldots,{\mathcal{F}}_{i}(y_{n}))
, and
\beta_{\infty}=\tilde{\beta}_{i}
, by using the formulas above for
\tilde{\beta}_{i}
and
{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}_{i}({\tilde{Y}})]
,
Equation

6.23
becomes:

v^{\infty}\left(1+\delta{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right]\right)=\overline{A}^{\top}F_{i}(I_{n}+F_{i})^{-1}\overline{A}v^{\infty}=\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})}\overline{A}^{\top}T\overline{A}v^{\infty},

(6.38)

where we recall that
T=\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{T}}(y_{1}),\ldots,{\mathcal{T}}(y_{n}))
.
Therefore, with choice
i
,
Equation

6.38
suggests that the GAMP iterate converges to an eigenvector of
\overline{D}=\overline{A}^{\top}T\overline{A}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})\left(1+\delta{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right]\right)
.
Moreover, when
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
,
Theorem

5.1
and
Remark

5.3
tell us that the leading eigenvalue of
\overline{D}
converges to:

\lambda_{i}(\overline{D})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})\left(1+\delta{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right]\right).

(6.39)

Therefore,
Equation

6.38
indicates that the GAMP iterates under each choices
1
and
2
converge to the eigenvectors corresponding to the two largest eigenvalues of
\overline{D}
, when
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})>\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
. We now make this claim rigorous.

6.2
Proof of
Theorem

3.1

Consider the GAMP iteration in
Equation

6.17
for
t\geq 2
. By substituting the expression for
u^{t}
in the
v^{t+1}
update, the iteration can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle u^{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}\,\beta_{t}}\left(\overline{A}v^{t}\,-\,Fu^{t-1}\right),\qquad v^{t+1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}}\left[\left(\overline{A}^{\top}F\overline{A}-\delta{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}({\tilde{Y}})]\,I_{d}\right)v^{t}\,-\,\overline{A}^{\top}F^{2}u^{t-1}\right].

(6.40)

In the remainder of the proof, we will assume that
t\geq 2
. Define

\displaystyle e_{1}^{t}

\displaystyle=u^{t}-u^{t-1},

(6.41)

\displaystyle e_{2}^{t}

\displaystyle=v^{t+1}-v^{t}.

(6.42)

By combining
Equation

6.41
with
Equation

6.40
, we have

u^{t-1}=(F+\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}I_{n})^{-1}\overline{A}v^{t}-\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}(F+\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}I_{n})^{-1}e_{1}^{t}.

(6.43)

Substituting the expression for
u^{t-1}
in
Equation

6.43
into
Equation

6.40
and recalling from
Equations

6.34
and
6.35
that
\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathcal{F}}(\widetilde{Y})\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathcal{F}}(Y)\right]
, we obtain:

\displaystyle v^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\left(\overline{A}^{\top}F(F+\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}I_{n})^{-1}\overline{A}-\frac{\sqrt{\delta}{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}(Y)]}{\beta_{t}}\,I_{d}\right)v^{t}+\overline{A}^{\top}F^{2}(F+\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}I_{n})^{-1}e_{1}^{t}

\displaystyle=\left(\overline{A}^{\top}F(F+I_{n})^{-1}\overline{A}-\delta{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}(Y)]\,I_{d}\right)v^{t}+(1-\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t})\overline{A}^{\top}F(F+I_{n})^{-1}(F+\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}I_{n})^{-1}\overline{A}v^{t}

\displaystyle\quad+\delta{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}(Y)]\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}}\right)v^{t}+\overline{A}^{\top}F^{2}(F+\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}I_{n})^{-1}e_{1}^{t}.

(6.44)

Let

e_{3}^{t}=\left(\overline{A}^{\top}F(F+I_{n})^{-1}\overline{A}-(\delta{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}(Y)]+1)\,I_{d}\right)v^{t}.

(6.45)

Using this in
Equation

6.44
along with
Equation

6.42
, we obtain

\begin{split}e_{3}^{t}&=e_{2}^{t}-(1-\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t})\overline{A}^{\top}F(F+I_{n})^{-1}(F+\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}I_{n})^{-1}\overline{A}v^{t}\\
&\quad-\delta{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}(Y)]\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}}\right)v^{t}-\overline{A}^{\top}F^{2}(F+\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}I_{n})^{-1}e_{1}^{t}.\end{split}

(6.46)

We now prove the two claims of
Theorem

3.1
via choices 1 and 2, respectively.
All the limits in the remainder of the proof hold almost surely, so we won’t specify this explicitly.

6.2.1
Proof of
Equation

3.7

Consider the GAMP algorithm with choice 1, as defined in
Equation

6.24
. With
{\mathcal{F}}(y)={\mathcal{F}}_{1}(y)
, we have:

F(F+I_{n})^{-1}=\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})}T,\qquad{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}(Y)]={\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right].

(6.47)

Recalling the notation
\overline{D}=\overline{A}^{\top}T\overline{A}
, let us decompose
v^{t}
into a component in the direction of
v_{1}(\overline{D})
plus an orthogonal component
r_{1}^{t}
:

v^{t}=\xi_{1,t}\,v_{1}(\overline{D})+r_{1}^{t},

(6.48)

where
\xi_{1,t}={\langle}v^{t},v_{1}(\overline{D}){\rangle}
. Substituting
Equation

6.48
in the definition of
e_{3}^{t}
in
Equation

6.45
and using
Equation

6.47
, we obtain

\left(\frac{\overline{D}}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})}-{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{\delta{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}+1\right]I_{d}\right)r_{1}^{t}\\
=e_{3}^{t}\,+\,\xi_{1,t}\left(\delta{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right]+1-\frac{\lambda_{1}(\overline{D})}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})}\right)v_{1}(\overline{D}).

(6.49)

The idea of the proof is to prove that
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\lim\limits_{d\to\infty}\|r_{1}^{t}\|_{2}^{2}/d=0
, which from
Equation

6.48
implies that the GAMP iterate is aligned with
v_{1}(\overline{D})
in the limit. To show this, we first claim that for all sufficiently large
n
:

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\frac{\overline{D}}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})}-{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{\delta{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}+1\right]I_{d}\right)r_{1}^{t}\right\|_{2}\geq C\left\|r_{1}^{t}\right\|_{2},

(6.50)

for some constant
C>0
that does not depend on
n
. We then consider the right side of
Equation

6.49
and show that under choice 1:

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{d\to\infty}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\left\|e_{3}^{t}\,+\,\xi_{1,t}\left(\delta{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right]+1-\frac{\lambda_{1}(\overline{D})}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})}\right)v_{1}(\overline{D})\right\|_{2}=0.

(6.51)

We now derive the result in
Equation

3.7

using
Equations

6.50
and
6.51
, deferring the proofs of these claims to the end of the section. Using
Equations

6.50
and
6.51
in
Equation

6.49
, we have that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\left\|r_{1}^{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{d}=0.

(6.52)

From the decomposition of
v^{t}
in
Equation

6.48
, we have

\left\|v^{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\xi_{1,t}^{2}\,+\,\left\|r_{1}^{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}\,,

(6.53)

since
r_{1}^{t}
is orthogonal to
v_{1}(\overline{D})
and
\left\|v_{1}(\overline{D})\right\|_{2}=1
. From
Proposition

6.1
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\left\|v^{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{d}={\mathbb E}[V_{t}^{2}]=\beta_{t}^{2},\qquad t\geq 1.

(6.54)

Moreover, from
Equation

6.33
and
Equation

6.36
, under choice 1,
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\beta_{t}^{2}={\tilde{\beta}}_{1}^{2}=\frac{1}{\delta}
. Therefore,

\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\left\|v^{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{d}=\frac{1}{\delta}.

(6.55)

Combining this with
Equations

6.53
and
6.52
yields

\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\xi_{1,t}^{2}}{d}=\frac{1}{\delta}.

(6.56)

Using
Equations

6.56
and
6.52
in
Equation

6.48
, and recalling the definition of
x^{\mathrm{spec}}_{1}
from the statement of
Theorem

3.1
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\|\sqrt{\delta}\,v^{t}-x_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{d}}=0.

(6.57)

For any
{\rm PL}(2)
function
\Psi:{\mathbb R}^{3}\to{\mathbb R}
, by an application of Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have that
[
FVRS22
, Lemma 7.24]

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Psi(\overline{x}^{*}_{1,i},x^{\mathrm{lin}}_{i},x^{\mathrm{spec}}_{1,i})-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Psi(\overline{x}^{*}_{1,i},x^{\mathrm{lin}}_{i},\sqrt{\delta}v^{t}_{i})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{\|\sqrt{\delta}v^{t}-x_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{d}}\left(1+\frac{\|\overline{x}_{1}^{*}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{d}}+\frac{\|x^{\mathrm{lin}}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{d}}+\frac{\|x_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{d}}+\frac{\|v^{t}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{d}}\right).

(6.58)

We have that
\|\overline{x}_{1}^{*}\|_{2}=\|x^{\mathrm{lin}}\|_{2}=\|x_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\|_{2}=\sqrt{d}
, by the definitions in the theorem statement. Therefore, using
Equations

6.55
and
6.57
in
Equation

6.58
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\,\left|\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Psi(\overline{x}^{*}_{1,i},x^{\mathrm{lin}}_{i},x^{\mathrm{spec}}_{1,i})-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Psi(\overline{x}^{*}_{1,i},x^{\mathrm{lin}}_{i},\sqrt{\delta}v^{t}_{i})\right|=0.

(6.59)

Recall from
Equation

6.17
that the GAMP iterate
v^{1}=\widehat{x}^{\mathrm{lin}}
, and
x^{\mathrm{lin}}=\sqrt{d}\,\widehat{x}^{\mathrm{lin}}/\left\|\widehat{x}^{\mathrm{lin}}\right\|_{2}
. From
Proposition

6.1
, we have that

\lim\limits_{d\to\infty}\frac{\left\|\widehat{x}^{\mathrm{lin}}\right\|_{2}}{d}=\sqrt{{\mathbb E}[V_{1}^{2}]}
. Using

Proposition

6.1
again, we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Psi\left(\overline{x}^{*}_{1,i},x^{\mathrm{lin}}_{i},\sqrt{\delta}v^{t}_{i}\right)=\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi\left(X_{1},\,\frac{V_{1}}{\sqrt{{\mathbb E}[V_{1}^{2}]}},\,\sqrt{\delta}V_{t}\right)\right].

(6.60)

From the definitions of
V_{1},V_{t}
in
Equation

6.4
, and the state evolution equations for choice 1 in
Equations

6.26
and
6.27
, we have

\displaystyle V_{1}=\chi_{1,1}X_{1}+\chi_{2,1}X_{2}+W_{V,1},\quad V_{t}=\chi_{1,t}X_{1}\,+\,W_{V,t},\quad t\geq 2.

(6.61)

Here
W_{V,1}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\,\delta{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{L}}({\tilde{Y}})^{2}])
and
W_{V,t}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\sigma_{V,t}^{2})
are independent of
(X_{1},X_{2})
, and from
Equations

6.10
and
6.15
, their covariance is given by

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[W_{V,1}W_{V,t}\right]=\delta\mathbb{E}\left[U_{t-1}{\mathcal{L}}({\tilde{Y}}){\mathcal{F}}({\tilde{Y}})\right]=\delta\alpha\mu_{1,t-1}\mathbb{E}\left[G{\mathcal{L}}(Y){\mathcal{F}}(Y)\right],\end{split}

(6.62)

where in the last line we have used that
\mu_{2,t-1}=0
under choice 1.
Hence, for
t\geq 2
,
Equation

6.60
becomes

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Psi\left(\overline{x}^{*}_{1,i},x^{\mathrm{lin}}_{i},\sqrt{\delta}v^{t}_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi\left(X_{1},\,\frac{\chi_{1,1}X_{1}+\chi_{2,1}X_{2}+W_{V,1}}{\sqrt{\chi_{1,1}^{2}+\chi_{2,1}^{2}+\delta{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{L}}(Y)^{2}]}},\,\sqrt{\delta}(\chi_{1,t}X_{1}+W_{V,t})\right)\right].

(6.63)

To obtain the result in
Equation

3.7
, we take
t\to\infty
on both sides above and show that

\begin{split}&\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi\left(X_{1},\,\frac{\chi_{1,1}X_{1}+\chi_{2,1}X_{2}+W_{V,1}}{\sqrt{\chi_{1,1}^{2}+\chi_{2,1}^{2}+\delta{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{L}}(Y)^{2}]}},\,\sqrt{\delta}(\chi_{1,t}X_{1}+W_{V,t})\right)\right]\\
&=\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi\left(X_{1},\,\frac{\chi_{1,1}X_{1}+\chi_{2,1}X_{2}+W_{V,1}}{\sqrt{\chi_{1,1}^{2}+\chi_{2,1}^{2}+\delta{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{L}}(Y)^{2}]}},\,\sqrt{\delta}(\tilde{\chi}_{1}X_{1}+W_{V,\infty})\right)\right],\end{split}

(6.64)

where
(W_{V,1},W_{V,\infty})
are jointly Gaussian with

W_{V,\infty}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\tilde{\sigma}_{1}^{2}),\qquad\mathbb{E}\left[W_{V,1}W_{V,\infty}\right]=\tilde{\chi}_{1}\delta\alpha\mathbb{E}\left[G{\mathcal{L}}(Y){\mathcal{F}}(Y)\right].

(6.65)

Here
\tilde{\chi}_{1}
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{1}^{2}
are given by

Equation

6.37
. Using
Equation

6.33
, we have

\begin{split}&\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[W_{V,t}^{2}\right]=\lim_{t\to\infty}\sigma_{V,t}^{2}=\tilde{\sigma}_{1}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\left[W_{V,\infty}^{2}\right],\\
&\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[W_{V,1}W_{V,t}\right]=\delta\alpha\mathbb{E}\left[G{\mathcal{L}}(Y){\mathcal{F}}(Y)\right]\lim_{t\to\infty}\mu_{1,t-1}=\tilde{\chi}_{1}\delta\alpha\mathbb{E}\left[G{\mathcal{L}}(Y){\mathcal{F}}(Y)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[W_{V,1}W_{V,\infty}\right],\end{split}

(6.66)

where in the second line, we have used the formula for
\mu_{1,t-1}
from
Equation

6.27
and that
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\beta_{t}=1/\sqrt{\delta}
(from
Equation

6.36
).
Equation

6.66
implies that the sequence of zero mean jointly Gaussian pairs
(W_{V,1},W_{V,t})_{t\geq 1}
converges in distribution to the jointly Gaussian pair
(W_{V,1},W_{V,\infty})
. To show
Equation

6.64
, we use
Lemma

I.7
in
Appendix

I
. We apply this result taking
Q_{t}
to be the distribution of

\left(X_{1},\,\frac{\chi_{1,1}X_{1}+\chi_{2,1}X_{2}+W_{V,1}}{\sqrt{\chi_{1,1}^{2}+\chi_{2,1}^{2}+\delta{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{L}}(Y)^{2}]}},\,\sqrt{\delta}(\chi_{1,t}X_{1}+W_{V,t})\right),\

and
Q
to be the distribution of

\left(X_{1},\,\frac{\chi_{1,1}X_{1}+\chi_{2,1}X_{2}+W_{V,1}}{\sqrt{\chi_{1,1}^{2}+\chi_{2,1}^{2}+\delta{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{L}}(Y)^{2}]}},\,\sqrt{\delta}(\tilde{\chi}_{1}X_{1}+W_{V,\infty})\right).

Since
\chi_{1,t}\to\tilde{\chi}_{1}
and the limits in
Equation

6.66
hold, the sequence
(Q_{t})_{t\geq 2}
converges weakly to
Q
. In our case,
\Psi:{\mathbb R}^{3}\to{\mathbb R}
is PL(
2
), and therefore
\Psi(a,b,c)\leq C^{\prime}(1+\left|a\right|^{2}+\left|b\right|^{2}+\left|c\right|^{2})
, for all
(a,b,c)\in{\mathbb R}^{3}
for some constant
C^{\prime}
. Choosing
h(a,b,c)=\left|a\right|^{2}+\left|b\right|^{2}+\left|c\right|^{2}
, we have
\frac{\left|\Psi\right|}{1+h}\leq C^{\prime}
. Furthermore,
\int h\,{\rm d}Q_{t}
is a linear combination of
\{\chi_{1,t}^{2},\sigma_{V,t}^{2}\}
, with coefficients that do not depend on
t
. The integral
\int h\,{\rm d}Q
has the same form, except that
\chi_{1,t},\sigma_{V,t}
are replaced by
\tilde{\chi}_{1},\tilde{\sigma}_{1}
, respectively. Since
\chi_{1,t}\to\tilde{\chi}_{1}
,
\sigma_{V,t}\to\tilde{\sigma}_{1}
, we have that
\lim_{t\to\infty}\int h\,{\rm d}Q_{t}=\int h\,{\rm d}Q
.
Therefore, by applying
Lemma

I.7
in
Appendix

I
, we have that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\int\Psi\,{\rm d}Q_{t}=\int\Psi\,{\rm d}Q,

(6.67)

which is equivalent to
Equation

6.64
. From
Equation

6.37
, we recall that
\tilde{\chi}_{1}=\frac{\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}}{\sqrt{\delta}}
,
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}_{1}=\frac{1-(\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}})^{2}}{\delta}
. Using these and the formulas for
\chi_{1,1},\chi_{2,1}
from
Equation

6.26
in
Equation

6.64
, and taking
t\to\infty
in
Equation

6.63
yields the result in
Equation

3.7
.

It remains to prove
Equations

6.50
and
6.51
.

Proof of
Equation

6.50
.

We recall from
Theorem

5.1
and
Remark

5.3
that when
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
,
the top eigenvalue of
\overline{D}\coloneqq\overline{A}^{\top}T\overline{A}
converges almost surely to

\begin{split}&\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{1}(\overline{D})=\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})\left(1+\delta{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right]\right).\\
\end{split}

(6.68)

Moreover, when
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
,
Theorem

5.1

also guarantees a strict separation between the first and second eigenvalues, i.e.,

\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{1}(\overline{D})>\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{2}(\overline{D})=\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2});\delta)\geq\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{3}(\overline{D})=\zeta(\overline{\lambda}(\delta);\delta).

(6.69)

Let

\displaystyle M_{1}\coloneqq\frac{\overline{D}}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})}-{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{\delta{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}+1\right]I_{d}.

(6.70)

As
M_{1}
is symmetric, it can be written as
M_{1}=Q\Lambda Q^{\top}
, with
Q
an orthogonal matrix consisting of the eigenvectors of
M_{1}
and
\Lambda
a diagonal matrix with the eigenvalues. Note that the eigenvectors of
M_{1}
are the same as those of
\overline{D}
and its eigenvalues are:

\lambda_{i}(M_{1})=\frac{\lambda_{i}(\overline{D})}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})}-{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{\delta{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}+1\right],\quad i=1,\ldots,d.

(6.71)

Since
r_{1}^{t}
is orthogonal to
v_{1}(\overline{D})=v_{1}(M_{1})
, we have
M_{1}r_{1}^{t}=Q\Lambda^{\prime}Q^{\top}r_{1}^{t}
, where
\Lambda^{\prime}
is obtained from
\Lambda
by replacing
\lambda_{1}(M_{1})
with any other value. Here we replace
\lambda_{1}(M_{1})
by
\lambda_{2}(M_{1})
. We therefore have

\displaystyle\left\|M_{1}r_{1}^{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|Q\Lambda^{\prime}Q^{\top}r_{1}^{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}\geq\left\|r_{1}^{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}\,\min_{s\in{\mathbb S}^{d-1}}\left\|Q\Lambda^{\prime}Q^{\top}s\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|r_{1}^{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}\,\min_{s\in{\mathbb S}^{d-1}}\langle s,Q(\Lambda^{\prime})^{2}Q^{\top}s\rangle=\left\|r_{1}^{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}\,\lambda_{d}(Q(\Lambda^{\prime})^{2}Q^{\top}),

(6.72)

where the last equality follows from the variational characterization of the smallest eigenvalue of a symmetric matrix (Courant–Fischer theorem). Note that

\lambda_{d}(Q(\Lambda^{\prime})^{2}Q^{\top})=\lambda_{d}((\Lambda^{\prime})^{2})=\min_{i\in\{2,\ldots,d\}}\lambda_{i}(M_{1})^{2}.

(6.73)

From the formula for
\lambda_{i}(M_{1})
in
Equation

6.71
and the limiting eigenvalues of
\overline{D}
in
Equations

6.68
and
6.69
, we have

\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{1}(M_{1})=0,\qquad\lim_{d\to\infty}\,\min_{i\in\{2,\ldots,d\}}\lambda_{i}(M_{1})^{2}=C>0,

(6.74)

for a universal constant
C
. Combining
Equations

6.72
,
6.73
and
6.74
shows that the lower bound in
Equation

6.50
holds for all sufficiently large
n
.

Proof of
Equation

6.51
.

Since
\left\|v_{1}(\overline{D})\right\|_{2}=1
, by the triangle inequality we have

\displaystyle\left\|e_{3}^{t}\,+\,\xi_{1,t}\left(\delta{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right]+1-\frac{\lambda_{1}(\overline{D})}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})}\right)v_{1}(\overline{D})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|e_{3}^{t}\right\|_{2}\,+\,\left|\xi_{1,t}\right|\cdot\left|\delta{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right]+1-\frac{\lambda_{1}(\overline{D})}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})}\right|.

(6.75)

From
Equations

6.56
and
6.68
we have:

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{d\to\infty}\,\frac{\left|\xi_{1,t}\right|}{\sqrt{d}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}},\qquad\lim_{d\to\infty}\,\left|\delta{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right]+1-\frac{\lambda_{1}(\overline{D})}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})}\right|=0.

(6.76)

Therefore, the second term in
Equation

6.75
converges to
0
.
For the term
\left\|e_{3}^{t}\right\|_{2}
, using the triangle inequality in the expression in
Equation

6.46
we obtain:

\begin{split}\left\|e_{3}^{t}\right\|_{2}\leq&\,\left\|e_{2}^{t}\right\|_{2}+\left|1-\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}\right|\left\|\overline{A}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\left\|F(F+I_{n})^{-1}(F+\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}I_{n})^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\left\|\overline{A}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\left\|v^{t}\right\|_{2}\\
&+\delta\left|{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}(Y)]\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}}\right)\right|\left\|v^{t}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\overline{A}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\left\|F^{2}(F+\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}I_{n})^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\left\|e_{1}^{t}\right\|_{2}.\end{split}

(6.77)

Here we have used the fact that
\left\|Mv\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|M\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\left\|v\right\|_{2}
for any matrix
M
and vector
v
, and that the operator norm is sub-multiplicative.

Since
\overline{A}
is i.i.d. Gaussian, its operator norm is bounded almost surely as
n
grows. With
{\mathcal{F}}(y)
given by choice 1 in
Equation

6.24
, the diagonal matrices in
Equation

6.77
become:

\displaystyle F(F+I_{n})^{-1}(F+\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}I_{n})^{-1}=\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})}\,T[\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})I_{n}-T][\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}I_{n}+(1-\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t})T]^{-1},

\displaystyle F^{2}(F+\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}I_{n})^{-1}=T^{2}[\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})I_{n}-T]^{-1}[\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}I_{n}+(1-\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t})T]^{-1}.

(6.78)

Recalling that
\beta_{t}>0
for
t>0
,
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\sqrt{\delta}\beta_{t}=1
and that
{\mathcal{T}}(\cdot)
is bounded, the operator norms of the diagonal matrices in
Equation

6.78
are both bounded.

Proposition

6.1
and
Equation

6.33
together imply that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\left\|v^{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{d}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\beta_{t}^{2}=\frac{1}{\delta}.

(6.79)

Recalling that
e_{1}^{t}=u^{t}-u^{t-1}
and
e_{2}^{t}=v^{t+1}-v^{t}
, using
Proposition

6.1
and
Equation

6.33
we can also show
[
MTV22
, Lemma 5.3]
that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\left\|e_{1}^{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{n}=0,\qquad\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\left\|e_{2}^{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{d}=0.

(6.80)

Using
Equations

6.78
,
6.79
and
6.80
in
Equation

6.77
shows that
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\lim\limits_{d\to\infty}\left\|e_{3}^{t}\right\|_{2}/\sqrt{d}=0
which, together with
Equations

6.75
and
6.76
, completes the proof of
Equation

6.51
.

6.2.2
Proof of
Equation

3.8

With choice 2, as defined in
Equation

6.25
, we have
{\mathcal{F}}(y)={\mathcal{F}}_{2}(y)
, which yields

F(F+I_{n})^{-1}=\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})}T,\qquad{\mathbb E}[{\mathcal{F}}(Y)]={\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right].

(6.81)

We decompose the GAMP iterate
v^{t}
into a component in the direction of
v_{2}(\overline{D})
plus an orthogonal component
r_{2}^{t}
:

v^{t}=\xi_{2,t}v_{2}(\overline{D})+r_{2}^{t},

(6.82)

where
\xi_{2,t}={\langle}v^{t},v_{2}(\overline{D}){\rangle}
. Substituting
Equation

6.82
in the definition of
e_{3}^{t}
in
Equation

6.45
and using
Equation

6.81
, we obtain

\left(\frac{\overline{D}}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})}-{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{\delta{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}+1\right]I_{d}\right)r_{2}^{t}\\
=e_{3}^{t}\,+\,\xi_{2,t}\left(1+\delta{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right]-\frac{\lambda_{2}(\overline{D})}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})}\right)v_{2}(\overline{D}).

(6.83)

We show that
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\lim\limits_{d\to\infty}\|r_{2}^{t}\|^{2}/d=0
, which from
Equation

6.82
implies that the GAMP iterate is aligned with
v_{2}(\overline{D})
in the limit. To show this, we first claim that for all sufficiently large
n
:

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\frac{\overline{D}}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})}-{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{\delta{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}+1\right]I_{d}\right)r_{2}^{t}\right\|_{2}\geq C\left\|r_{2}^{t}\right\|_{2},

(6.84)

for some constant
C>0
. We then show that under choice 2:
