\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{d\to\infty}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\left\|e_{3}^{t}\,+\,\xi_{2,t}\left(\delta{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right]+1-\frac{\lambda_{2}(\overline{D})}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})}\right)v_{2}(\overline{D})\right\|_{2}=0.

(6.85)

Given the claims in
Equations

6.84
and
6.85
, the result in
Equation

3.8
is obtained using the same steps as
Equation

6.52
to (
6.67
), by replacing
\overline{x}_{1}^{*},x_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}},r_{1}^{t},\alpha,\xi_{1,t},\tilde{\chi}_{1},\tilde{\sigma}_{1}
with
\overline{x}_{2}^{*},x_{2}^{\mathrm{spec}},r_{2}^{t},(1-\alpha),\xi_{2,t},\tilde{\chi}_{2},\tilde{\sigma}_{2}
, respectively.

The proof of
Equation

6.84
is along the same lines as that of
Equation

6.50
. Other than replacing notation as above, the only change is in the argument from
Equation

6.70
to (
6.74
). Here we define the matrix
M_{2}\coloneqq\frac{\overline{D}}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})}-{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{\delta{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}+1\right]I_{d}
, which can be written as
M_{2}=Q\bar{\Lambda}Q^{\top}
, where
\bar{\Lambda}
is a diagonal matrix containing the eigenvalues of
M_{2}
, and
Q
is an orthogonal matrix with the eigenvectors. The eigenvectors of
M_{2}
are the same as those of
\overline{D}
and its eigenvalues are:

\lambda_{i}(M_{2})=\frac{\lambda_{i}(\overline{D})}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})}-{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{\delta{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}+1\right],\quad i=1,\ldots,d.

(6.86)

Theorem

5.1
and
Remark

5.3
guarantee that when
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})>\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
, there is strict separation between the top three
three eigenvalues of
\overline{D}\coloneqq\overline{A}^{\top}T\overline{A}
. The limits of these eigenvalues are:

\begin{split}\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{1}(\overline{D})&=\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})\left(1+\delta{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right]\right)\\
&>\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{2}(\overline{D})=\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})\left(1+\delta{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{2})-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right]\right)\\
&>\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{3}(\overline{D})=\bar{\lambda}(\delta)\left(1+\delta{\mathbb E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\bar{\lambda}(\delta)-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right]\right).\end{split}

(6.87)

Since
r_{2}^{t}
is orthogonal to
v_{2}(\overline{D})=v_{2}(M_{2})
, we have
M_{2}r_{2}^{t}=Q\bar{\Lambda}^{\prime}Q^{\top}r_{1}^{t}
, where
\bar{\Lambda}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\bar{\Lambda}
by replacing the
second
eigenvalue
\lambda_{2}(M_{2})
with any other value. Here we replace
\lambda_{2}(M_{2})
by
\lambda_{1}(M_{2})
. Then, using the eigenvalue limits in
Equation

6.87
together with arguments analogous to
Equations

6.72
,
6.73
and
6.74
, we obtain:

\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{2}(M_{2})=0,\qquad\lim_{d\to\infty}\,\min_{i\neq 2}\lambda_{i}(M_{2})^{2}=C>0,

(6.88)

for a universal constant
C
.

The proof of
Equation

6.85
is essentially identical to that of
Equation

6.51
, and is omitted. This completes the proof of
Theorem

3.1
.

Remark 6.2

(Adapting the argument to
\alpha=1/2
)
.

To obtain the result mentioned in
Remark

3.4
, we
analyze a pair of GAMP algorithms with the same design of denoisers and initializers as in choices 1 and 2. In particular, one can show that
v^{t}
converges to a pair of linearly independent vectors in the span of
v_{1}(\overline{D})
and
v_{2}(\overline{D})
under choices 1 and 2.
To prove the claim, under choice 1, we decompose
v^{t}
into the projection onto
\spn\left\{v_{1}(\overline{D}),v_{2}(\overline{D})\right\}
and the orthogonal component
r_{1}^{t}
.
Via a similar analysis,
Equation

6.52
can be shown to hold, provided that
\lambda^{*}(\delta/2)>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
, since this condition ensures the existence of a spectral gap (cf.
Remark

5.2
).
Hence,
v^{t}
converges to a vector in
\spn\left\{v_{1}(\overline{D}),v_{2}(\overline{D})\right\}
.
Furthermore,
Equation

6.60
continues to hold by state evolution (
Proposition

6.1
).
As a result,
v^{t}
converges to a vector
{\tilde{v}}_{1}
whose limiting empirical distribution has the law of
\tilde{\chi}_{1}X_{1}+W_{V,\infty}
, with
W_{V,\infty}
independent of
(X_{1},X_{2})
.
Similarly, under choice 2,
v^{t}
converges to another vector
{\tilde{v}}_{2}
in
\spn\left\{v_{1}(\overline{D}),v_{2}(\overline{D})\right\}
whose limiting empirical distribution has the law of
\tilde{\chi}_{2}X_{2}+W_{V,\infty}^{\prime}
, with
W_{V,\infty}^{\prime}
independent of
(X_{1},X_{2})
. By recognizing that the inner products of
{\tilde{v}}_{1},{\tilde{v}}_{2}
with
x_{1}^{*}
differ, one readily obtains that
{\tilde{v}}_{1},{\tilde{v}}_{2}
are linearly independent.
Therefore, in the high-dimensional limit, the GAMP iterates recover
\spn\left\{v_{1}(\overline{D}),v_{2}(\overline{D})\right\}
.
At this point, we can find the vector in
\spn\left\{v_{1}(\overline{D}),v_{2}(\overline{D})\right\}
with the desired limiting joint law as in
Equations

3.7
and
3.8
by matching its correlation with the linear estimator
x^{\mathrm{lin}}
via
Equation

3.9
.
This last step of grid search can be effectively carried out when the overlap attained by
x^{\mathrm{lin}}
is non-zero, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}\left[G{\mathcal{L}}(Y)\right]\neq 0
.

7
Discussion

We conclude with a discussion concerning:
(i)
the universality of the predictions obtained with a Gaussian design matrix (cf.
Item

(A4)
),
(ii)
the extension of our results to mixed GLMs with more than two signals, and
(iii)
lower bounds on inference in mixed models.

Universality beyond the Gaussian design matrix.

A natural question is whether the predictions obtained under an i.i.d. Gaussian design are valid for a broader class of designs. This topic has been investigated in the random matrix theory literature
[
TV11
,
EYY12
,
TCSV10
,
Far11
,
AF14
]
, and a recent line of research has focused on approximate message passing algorithms
[
BLM15
,
CL21
,
DB22
,
WZF22
,
DLS22
,
DSL22
]
. We note that none of these results is directly applicable to our setting and the problem remains open also in the non-mixed (i.e.,
\alpha=1
) setup. However, the aforementioned body of work suggests that the Gaussian predictions may hold for much more general – even “almost deterministic” – design matrices.

Mixed GLM with multiple components.

The results of this work are presented for the mixed GLM with two components, i.e., a model with two signals
x_{1}^{*},x_{2}^{*}
mixed by a binary variable
\eta
. However, both our RMT and AMP tools are well suited to handle mixed GLMs with
multiple
components. We now briefly sketch how our main
Theorem

3.1
can be extended to the multiple-component setting. The other results that follow from the master theorem (overlaps for linear, spectral and combined estimators, and their optimization) can be generalized in a similar fashion.

Let
x_{1}^{*},\cdots,x_{\ell}^{*}\in{\mathbb R}^{d}
be
\ell
signal vectors, and let the observation
y=(y_{1},\cdots,y_{n})\in{\mathbb R}^{n}
be generated as

\displaystyle y_{i}

\displaystyle=q\left(\left\langle a_{i},x_{\upsilon_{i}}^{*}\right\rangle,\varepsilon_{i}\right)\in{\mathbb R}.

Here, the latent vector
\underline{\upsilon}=(\upsilon_{1},\cdots,\upsilon_{n})
is a sequence of i.i.d. mixing random variables such that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\upsilon_{i}=j\right]

\displaystyle=\alpha_{j},\quad\forall\,i\in[n],\,\,\forall\,j\in[\ell].

We assume that
x_{1}^{*},\cdots,x_{\ell}^{*}
are i.i.d. and uniformly distributed on the unit sphere, and that
1>\alpha_{1}>\alpha_{2}>\cdots>\alpha_{\ell}>0
(which corresponds to
Items

(A1)
and
(A2)
). We also impose our previous
Items

(A3)
,
(A4)
and
(A5)
, and assume that
\ell
is a constant (independent of
n,d
).

For
i\in[\ell]
, let
\delta_{i}\coloneqq\alpha_{i}\delta
and

\displaystyle n^{\mathrm{lin}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\alpha_{k}^{2}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[G{\mathcal{L}}(Y)\right]^{2}+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathcal{L}}(Y)^{2}\right]}{\delta}\right)^{1/2},

\displaystyle\rho_{i}^{\mathrm{lin}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\alpha_{i}\mathbb{E}\left[G{\mathcal{L}}(Y)\right]}{n^{\mathrm{lin}}},\qquad\rho_{i}^{\mathrm{spec}}\coloneqq\left(\frac{\frac{1}{\delta}-\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})-Z}\right)^{2}\right]}{\frac{1}{\delta}+\alpha_{i}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})-Z}\right)^{2}(G^{2}-1)\right]}\right)^{1/2}.

Here
Z={\mathcal{T}}(Y)
,
Y=q(G,\varepsilon)
, and
G\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
, as before. Then, under the same setting of
Theorem

3.1
with
\overline{x}_{i}^{*}
and
x_{i}^{\mathrm{spec}}
defined similarly for
i\in[\ell]
, we have that, if
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Psi(\overline{x}_{i,j}^{*},x_{j}^{\mathrm{lin}},x_{i,j}^{\mathrm{spec}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi\left(X_{i},\,\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\rho_{k}^{\mathrm{lin}}X_{k}+W^{\mathrm{lin}},\,\rho_{i}^{\mathrm{spec}}X_{i}+W_{i}^{\mathrm{spec}}\right)\right],

where
(X_{1},\cdots,X_{\ell})\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)^{\otimes\ell}
,
(W^{\mathrm{lin}},W_{i}^{\mathrm{spec}})
is independent of
(X_{1},\cdots,X_{\ell})
and is jointly Gaussian with zero mean and covariance given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(W^{\mathrm{lin}})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=1-\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}(\rho_{k}^{\mathrm{lin}})^{2},\;\mathbb{E}\left[(W_{i}^{\mathrm{spec}})^{2}\right]=1-(\rho_{i}^{\mathrm{spec}})^{2},\;\mathbb{E}\left[W^{\mathrm{lin}}W_{i}^{\mathrm{spec}}\right]=\frac{\alpha_{i}\rho_{i}^{\mathrm{spec}}}{n^{\mathrm{lin}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{G{\mathcal{L}}(Y)Z}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})-Z}\right].

The result on the eigenvalues of the spectral matrix
D
can be obtained by following the strategy detailed in
Section

5
. Indeed,
D
can be decomposed into the (asymptotically) free sum of the
\ell
components associated to each of the signals, and
[
BBCF17
, Theorem 2.1]
is well equipped to characterize its top
\ell+1
eigenvalues (see
Remark

G.2
in
Appendix

G
).
To derive the limiting joint empirical law of the
i
-th signal and the linear and spectral estimators, we can then run a GAMP algorithm similar to
Equation

6.15
with denoisers tailored for the
i
-th signal.
The condition
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
guarantees the existence of a spectral gap between the
i
-th largest eigenvalue of
D
and the rest of its spectrum, which in turn is leveraged to argue the convergence of GAMP to the desired eigenvector.
This yields results analogous to
Theorem

3.1
with
\alpha
underlying
Equation

3.7
therein replaced with
\alpha_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq\ell
.

Lower bounds for inference in mixed GLMs.

For the non-mixed setting (i.e.,
\alpha=1
), in addition to providing precise asymptotics for the spectral method,
[
MM19
]
also derives an
information-theoretic threshold

\delta^{\mathrm{it}}
such that, for
\delta<\delta^{\mathrm{it}}
,
no
estimation method can give a non-trivial estimate of the signal.
3
3

3

More formally, it is proved that the minimum mean squared error achieved by the Bayes-optimal estimator coincides with the error of a trivial estimator which always outputs the all-0 vector.
Furthermore, for the special case of noiseless phase retrieval, it is shown that
\delta^{\mathrm{it}}=1/2
, which matches the threshold of
\delta
above which the spectral method provides a strictly positive overlap.

For the mixed setting considered in this paper, denote by
\delta^{\mathrm{it}}_{1},\delta^{\mathrm{it}}_{2}
the information-theoretic thresholds corresponding to the two signals. Then, the following bounds trivially hold:

\displaystyle\delta^{\mathrm{it}}_{1}\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}\delta^{\mathrm{it}},\qquad\delta^{\mathrm{it}}_{2}\geq\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\delta^{\mathrm{it}}.

(7.1)

That is, for any
\delta<\frac{1}{\alpha}\delta^{\mathrm{it}}
(resp.
\delta<\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\delta^{\mathrm{it}}
), no method can achieve strictly positive overlap with
x_{1}^{*}
(resp.
x_{2}^{*}
).
To see this, it suffices to note that, if a genie reveals the values of the mixing variables
(\eta_{1},\cdots,\eta_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, then
the estimation problem given mixed data with aspect ratio
\delta
can be decoupled into two non-mixed ones with aspect ratios
\alpha\delta
and
(1-\alpha)\delta
, respectively.

Another approach for obtaining an information-theoretic lower bound is to generalize to the mixed setting the analysis in
[
MM19
]
, which is based on bounding the conditional entropy via the second moment method. However, if one pursues this strategy, the resulting thresholds do not compare favorably with the trivial bound in
Equation

7.1
(hence, their derivation is omitted). We also note that the approach based on the second moment method does not generally lead to a tight result. The exact asymptotics of the minimum mean squared error, and therefore, a tight information-theoretic lower bound for inference in GLMs were obtained in
[
BKM+19
]
.
Adapting the techniques of
[
BKM+19
]
to our mixed setting requires additional ideas beyond the scope of this paper, so it is left for future research.

As a final remark, let us contrast the trivial information-theoretic lower bounds in
Equation

7.1
with the spectral bounds mentioned in
Remark

3.8
, which are universal in the sense that they hold for
any
mixed GLM. In particular, we note that the former scale as
(1/\alpha,1/(1-\alpha))
, while the latter as
(1/\alpha^{2},1/(1-\alpha)^{2})
, which suggests a gap between what is achievable information-theoretically and algorithmically. The possibility of a statistical-computational trade-off is also suggested by the fact that, for
\alpha=1/2
and antipodal signals (
x_{1}^{*}=-x_{2}^{*}
), mixed linear regression reduces to phase retrieval, which is widely believed to have such a gap, see e.g.
[
MLKZ20
,
BB20
,
CMW20
,
AV23
]
. Closing the gap or understanding its fundamental nature remains an intriguing open question for future investigation.

Appendix A
Bayes-optimal combination (proof of
Corollary

3.2
)

In the proof, we only consider combined estimators for the estimation of
x_{1}^{*}
.
The arguments for the estimation of
x_{2}^{*}
are similar, and therefore omitted.
For any
C_{1}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
(the latter set is defined in
Equation

3.11
), consider the combined estimator
x_{1}^{\mathrm{comb}}\coloneqq C_{1}(x^{\mathrm{lin}},x_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}})
.
By
Theorem

3.1
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\left|\left\langle x_{1}^{\mathrm{comb}},x_{1}^{*}\right\rangle\right|}{\left\|x_{1}^{\mathrm{comb}}\right\|_{2}\left\|x_{1}^{*}\right\|_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left|\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}C_{1}(X^{\mathrm{lin}},X_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}})\right]\right|}{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[C_{1}(X^{\mathrm{lin}},X_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}})^{2}\right]}}.

almost surely.
Here we use the fact that
\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}^{2}\right]=1
which follows from
\overline{x}_{1}^{*}\in\sqrt{d}\,{\mathbb S}^{d-1}
.
Now, the optimality of the conditional expectation function
C_{1}^{*}
follows from the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality:

\displaystyle\frac{\left|\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}C_{1}(X^{\mathrm{lin}},X_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}})\right]\right|}{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[C_{1}(X^{\mathrm{lin}},X_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}})^{2}\right]}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}\,|\,X^{\mathrm{lin}},X_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\right]C_{1}(X^{\mathrm{lin}},X_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}})\right]\right|}{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[C_{1}(X^{\mathrm{lin}},X_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}})^{2}\right]}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}\,|\,X^{\mathrm{lin}},X_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\right]^{2}\right]},

(A.1)

with equality in
Equation

A.1
if
C_{1}=C_{1}^{*}
.

We then explicitly compute
\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}\,|\,X^{\mathrm{lin}},X_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\right]
from the joint distribution of
(X_{1},X^{\mathrm{lin}},X^{\mathrm{spec}})
given by
Equations

3.5
and
3.10
.
Under
Item

(A1)
, we have
(X_{1},X_{2})\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)^{\otimes 2}
.
Using this it can be verified that
(X_{1},X^{\mathrm{lin}},X^{\mathrm{spec}})
are jointly Gaussian with zero mean and the following covariance matrix:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}1&\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{lin}}&\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\\
\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{lin}}&1&\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{lin}}\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}+\mathbb{E}\left[W^{\mathrm{lin}}W_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\right]\\
\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}&\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{lin}}\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}+\mathbb{E}\left[W^{\mathrm{lin}}W_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\right]&1\end{bmatrix}.

Let
\nu_{1}=\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{lin}}\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}+\mathbb{E}\left[W^{\mathrm{lin}}W_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\right]
.
Using the covariance structure above, we obtain that
X_{1}
conditioned on
(X^{\mathrm{lin}},X_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}})
is a Gaussian random variable with mean

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{lin}}&\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&\nu_{1}\\
\nu_{1}&1\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}X^{\mathrm{lin}}\\
X_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\end{bmatrix}

and variance

\displaystyle\widetilde{\sigma}^{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 1-\begin{bmatrix}\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{lin}}&\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&\nu_{1}\\
\nu_{1}&1\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{lin}}\\
\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\end{bmatrix}.

Therefore, the Bayes-optimal combined estimator is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}\,|\,X^{\mathrm{lin}},X_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\right]

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mu}=\frac{1}{1-\nu_{1}^{2}}\left[\left(\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{lin}}-\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\nu_{1}\right)X^{\mathrm{lin}}+\left(\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}-\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{lin}}\nu_{1}\right)X_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\right],

which agrees with the expression in
Equation

3.13
.
Finally, the explicit formulas of the overlaps given by the Bayes-optimal combined estimators can be obtained from
Equations

A.1
and
3.13
via elementary algebraic manipulations.

Appendix B
Additional proofs for linear estimator

B.1
Optimization of overlap (proof of
Proposition

3.4
)

With the characterization of the limiting overlaps of the linear estimator
in
Corollary

3.3
, we can maximize them over the choice of the preprocessing function
{\mathcal{L}}\colon{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}
. For
i\geq 0
, let

\displaystyle m_{i}(y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[G^{i}p(y|G)\right],

(B.1)

where
G\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
and
p(y|g)
is defined in
Equation

2.2
.
Using
m_{0},m_{1}
, the squared limiting overlap between
\widehat{x}^{\mathrm{lin}}
and
x_{1}^{*}
in
Equation

3.14
can be expressed in the following way:

\frac{\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[G{\mathcal{L}}(Y)\right]^{2}}{(\alpha^{2}+(1-\alpha)^{2})\mathbb{E}\left[G{\mathcal{L}}(Y)\right]^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathcal{L}}(Y)^{2}\right]/\delta}=\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}+(1-\alpha)^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}\delta}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathcal{L}}(Y)^{2}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[G{\mathcal{L}}(Y)\right]^{2}}\right)^{-1}\\
=\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}+(1-\alpha)^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}\delta}\cdot\frac{\int_{\supp(Y)}m_{0}(y){\mathcal{L}}(y)^{2}\mathrm{d}y}{\left(\int_{\supp(Y)}m_{1}(y){\mathcal{L}}(y)\mathrm{d}y\right)^{2}}\right)^{-1},

(B.2)

provided
\int_{\supp(Y)}m_{1}(y){\mathcal{L}}(y)\mathrm{d}y\neq 0
and
\mathbb{E}\left[|G{\mathcal{L}}(Y)|\right]<\infty
.
The optimization of overlap can be formalized as the following maximization problem:

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{OL}_{1}^{\mathrm{lin}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{{\mathcal{L}}\colon{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}+(1-\alpha)^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}\delta}\cdot\frac{\int_{\supp(Y)}m_{0}(y){\mathcal{L}}(y)^{2}\mathrm{d}y}{\left(\int_{\supp(Y)}m_{1}(y){\mathcal{L}}(y)\mathrm{d}y\right)^{2}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\phantom{\coloneqq}\mathrm{\ s.t.}\int_{\supp(Y)}m_{1}(y){\mathcal{L}}(y)\mathrm{d}y\neq 0

(B.3)

\displaystyle\phantom{\coloneqq\mathrm{\ s.t.}}\mathbb{E}\left[|G{\mathcal{L}}(Y)|\right]<\infty.

(B.4)

Therefore, maximizing
\mathsf{OL}_{1}^{\mathrm{lin}}
is equivalent to solving the following minimization problem:

\displaystyle\mathsf{OPT}^{\mathrm{lin}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf_{{\mathcal{L}}\colon{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}}\frac{\int_{\supp(Y)}m_{0}(y){\mathcal{L}}(y)^{2}\mathrm{d}y}{\left(\int_{\supp(Y)}m_{1}(y){\mathcal{L}}(y)\mathrm{d}y\right)^{2}}\quad\mathrm{\ s.t.}\quad\text{\lx@cref{creftypepluralcap\lx@tilde refnum}{eqn:ol-lin-constraint-1} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{eqn:ol-lin-constraint-2}}.

This optimization problem has been studied in
[
MTV22
, Appendix C.1]
.
In particular, under the condition

\displaystyle\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{m_{1}(y)^{2}}{m_{0}(y)}\mathrm{d}y\in(0,\infty)

(B.5)

(which is equivalent to
Equation

3.15
),
we have

\mathsf{OPT}^{\mathrm{lin}}=\left(\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{m_{1}(y)^{2}}{m_{0}(y)}\mathrm{d}y\right)^{-1}
,
attained by
{\mathcal{L}}^{*}\colon{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}
defined as (cf.
[
MTV22
, Eqn. (C.4)]
)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}^{*}(y)=\frac{m_{1}(y)}{m_{0}(y)},

(B.6)

which satisfies
\int_{\supp(Y)}m_{1}(y){\mathcal{L}}^{*}(y)\mathrm{d}y>0
and
\mathbb{E}\left[|G{\mathcal{L}}^{*}(Y)|\right]<\infty
.

Therefore, the value of the original problem
\mathsf{OL}_{1}^{\mathrm{lin}}
we are interested in is given by

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{OL}_{1}^{\mathrm{lin}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}+(1-\alpha)^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}\delta}\cdot\frac{1}{\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{m_{1}(y)^{2}}{m_{0}(y)}\mathrm{d}y}\right)^{-1}.

(B.7)

Analogously, the optimal (over the choice of
{\mathcal{L}}\colon{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}
) limiting overlap between
\widehat{x}^{\mathrm{lin}}
and
x_{2}^{*}
equals

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{OL}_{2}^{\mathrm{lin}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}+(1-\alpha)^{2}}{(1-\alpha)^{2}}+\frac{1}{(1-\alpha)^{2}\delta}\cdot\frac{1}{\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{m_{1}(y)^{2}}{m_{0}(y)}\mathrm{d}y}\right)^{-1},

(B.8)

which is also achieved by
{\mathcal{L}}^{*}\colon{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}
defined in
Equation

B.6
.

B.2
Evaluation of overlap (proof of
Corollary

3.7
)

Mixed linear regression.

For the mixed linear regression model in
Equation

3.26
, the conditional law
p(y|g)
is given by

p(y|g)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{(y-g)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)
.
Using this, it is straightforward to verify that

\displaystyle m_{0}(y)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(1+\sigma^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2(1+\sigma^{2})}\right),

\displaystyle m_{1}(y)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\left[Gp(y|G)\right]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(1+\sigma^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2(1+\sigma^{2})}\right)\frac{y}{1+\sigma^{2}}.

Note that this model satisfies the regularity condition in
Equation

B.5
.
Indeed,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{m_{1}(y)^{2}}{m_{0}(y)}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(1+\sigma^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2(1+\sigma^{2})}\right)\left(\frac{y}{1+\sigma^{2}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}y=\frac{1}{1+\sigma^{2}}\in(0,\infty).

According to
Equation

B.6
, the optimal preprocessing function for linear estimator is
{\mathcal{L}}^{*}(y)=\frac{y}{1+\sigma^{2}}
,
which, by
Equations

B.7
and
B.8
, results in the optimal overlaps

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{OL}_{1}^{\mathrm{lin}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}+(1-\alpha)^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}+\frac{1+\sigma^{2}}{\alpha^{2}\delta}\right)^{-1},\quad\left(\mathsf{OL}_{2}^{\mathrm{lin}}\right)^{2}=\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}+(1-\alpha)^{2}}{(1-\alpha)^{2}}+\frac{1+\sigma^{2}}{(1-\alpha)^{2}\delta}\right)^{-1}.

Mixed phase retrieval.

For the mixed phase retrieval model in
Equation

3.27
, the conditional law
p(y|g)
is given by
p(y|g)\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{(y-|g|)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)
.
Using this, it can be verified that

\displaystyle m_{0}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(1+\sigma^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2(1+\sigma^{2})}\right)\left[1+\mathrm{erf}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}(1+\sigma^{2})}}\right)\right],\quad m_{1}(y)=0.

According to
Equation

B.2
, we see that the overlaps are constantly zero, regardless of the choice of the preprocessing function.

Appendix C
Additional proofs for spectral estimator (
Proposition

3.6
)

C.1
Optimization of spectral threshold

Let us consider weak recovery of
x_{1}^{*}
.
(The analysis for the recovery of
x_{2}^{*}
is completely analogous and is therefore omitted.) For a given preprocessing function
{\mathcal{T}}\colon{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}
, we know from
Corollary

3.5
and
Remark

3.7
that weak recovery of
x_{1}^{*}
is possible (i.e.,
\rho_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}>0
) when
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
.
From
Item

2
in
Lemma

I.3
, this condition is equivalent to
\varphi(\overline{\lambda}(\delta))>\zeta(\overline{\lambda}(\delta);\delta_{1})=\psi(\overline{\lambda}(\delta);\delta_{1})
, or more explicitly,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{\overline{\lambda}(\delta)-Z}(G^{2}-1)\right]

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{\delta_{1}}=\frac{1}{\alpha\delta}.

(C.1)

Here we recall that
Z={\mathcal{T}}(Y)
, and
\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
satisfies
\psi^{\prime}(\overline{\lambda}(\delta);\delta)=0
, or equivalently (
Lemma

I.2
), it is the solution to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z}{\overline{\lambda}(\delta)-Z}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta}.

(C.2)

Note that
Equation

C.1
assumes that

\overline{\lambda}(\delta)>0
, which is satisfied since
\overline{\lambda}(\delta)>\sup\supp({\mathcal{T}}(Y))
by definition (cf.
Equation

3.3
) and the RHS is strictly positive by
Item

(A7)
.
Due to scaling invariance, we claim that
\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
can be assumed to be
1
.
Indeed, both
Equations

C.1
and
C.2
depend on
{\mathcal{T}}\colon{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}
only through the ratio
\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{\overline{\lambda}(\delta)-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}
, therefore
any given
{\mathcal{T}}
and the corresponding
\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
derived via
Equation

C.2
can be replaced with
{\mathcal{T}}/\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
and
1
, respectively.

Equations

C.1
and
C.2
then become

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{1-Z}(G^{2}-1)\right]>\frac{1}{\alpha\delta},\qquad\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z}{1-Z}\right)^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{\delta}.

(C.3)

For convenience, let
\Gamma(y)\coloneqq\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(y)}{1-{\mathcal{T}}(y)}
.
The expectations in
Equation

C.3
can then be written as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z}{1-Z}\right)^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}{1-{\mathcal{T}}(Y)}\right)^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\Gamma(Y)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\supp(Y)}\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]\cdot\Gamma(y)^{2}\mathrm{d}y,

(C.4)

where
G\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
. Similarly

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{1-Z}(G^{2}-1)\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\supp(Y)}\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)(G^{2}-1)\right]\cdot\Gamma(y)\mathrm{d}y.

(C.5)

So the conditions in
Equation

C.3
can be further written as

\displaystyle\int_{\supp(Y)}\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]\cdot\Gamma(y)^{2}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta},

(C.6)

and

\displaystyle\int_{\supp(Y)}\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)(G^{2}-1)\right]\cdot\Gamma(y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{\alpha\delta}.

(C.7)

In the above form, one can apply Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to
Equation

C.7
given the equality condition in
Equation

C.6
. Hence, we can upper bound the LHS of
Equation

C.7
as

\displaystyle\int_{\supp(Y)}\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)(G^{2}-1)\right]\cdot

\displaystyle\Gamma(y)\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\supp(Y)}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]}\cdot\Gamma(y)\cdot\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)(G^{2}-1)\right]}{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]}}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\int_{\supp(Y)}\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]\cdot\Gamma(y)^{2}\mathrm{d}y}\cdot\sqrt{\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)(G^{2}-1)\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]}\mathrm{d}y}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}\cdot\frac{1}{\alpha\sqrt{\delta^{*}_{1}}},

(C.8)

where the last equality is obtained using
Equation

C.6
and by defining

\displaystyle\delta^{*}_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)(G^{2}-1)\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]}\mathrm{d}y}

(C.9)

Combining
Equations

C.7
and
C.8
, we obtain the condition
\delta>\delta^{*}_{1}
.

So far we have shown that if
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
under a preprocessing function
{\mathcal{T}}\colon{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}
, then it must be the case that
\delta>\delta^{*}_{1}
.
In what follows, we show that whenever
\delta>\delta^{*}_{1}
, one can find a preprocessing function
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}\colon{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}
that achieves equality in both
Equations

C.6
and
C.8
, guaranteeing that
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
.

Consider any
\delta>\delta^{*}_{1}
.
Our goal is to find a function
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{*}_{1}\colon{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(Y)}{1-\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(Y)}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta},

(C.10)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(Y)}{1-\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{*}_{1}(Y)}(G^{2}-1)\right]=\frac{1}{\alpha\sqrt{\delta}\sqrt{\delta^{*}_{1}}}>\frac{1}{\alpha\delta}.

(C.11)

Note that according to
Equation

C.10
, the corresponding
\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
associated with
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{*}_{1}
(to be constructed) is equal to
1
.
Constructing such a
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}
is equivalent to constructing a function
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}
, which we define via

\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(y)}{1-\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(y)}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\delta^{*}_{1}}{\delta}}\cdot\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(y)}{1-{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(y)}

(C.12)

for every
y\in{\mathbb R}
.
Now
Equations

C.10
and
C.11
are equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(Y)}{1-{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(Y)}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta^{*}_{1}},\qquad\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(Y)}{1-{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(Y)}(G^{2}-1)\right]=\frac{1}{\alpha\delta^{*}_{1}}.

(C.13)

Before proceeding, let us define the following functions for convenience:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}^{*}(y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(y)}{1-{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(y)},\qquad m_{0}(y)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right],\qquad m_{2}(y)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)G^{2}\right].

(C.14)

With the above notation,
\delta^{*}_{1}
in
Equation

C.9
can be written as

\displaystyle\delta^{*}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{(m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y))^{2}}{m_{0}(y)}\mathrm{d}y},

(C.15)

and the LHSs of
Equation

C.13
can be written as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(Y)}{1-{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(Y)}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}^{*}(y)^{2}m_{0}(y)\mathrm{d}y

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(Y)}{1-{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(Y)}(G^{2}-1)\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}^{*}(y)(m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y))\mathrm{d}y

respectively, using
Equations

C.4
and
C.5
.
Then,
Equation

C.13
becomes

\displaystyle\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}^{*}(y)^{2}m_{0}(y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\alpha^{2}\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{(m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y))^{2}}{m_{0}(y)}\mathrm{d}y

(C.16)

and

\displaystyle\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}^{*}(y)(m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y))\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\alpha\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{(m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y))^{2}}{m_{0}(y)}\mathrm{d}y.

(C.17)

By inspecting
Equations

C.16
and
C.17
, we conclude that the following choice of
\Gamma_{1}^{*}\colon{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}
meets both conditions:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}^{*}(y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\alpha\cdot\frac{m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y)}{m_{0}(y)}.

By
Equation

C.14
, this gives a choice of
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma_{1}^{*}(y)}{1+\Gamma_{1}^{*}(y)}=\frac{\alpha\cdot\frac{m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y)}{m_{0}(y)}}{1+\alpha\cdot\frac{m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y)}{m_{0}(y)}}=1-\frac{1}{\alpha\cdot\frac{m_{2}(y)}{m_{0}(y)}+(1-\alpha)}.

(C.18)

Using the relation in
Equation

C.12
, we can then determine
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(y)
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(y)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{1+\sqrt{\frac{\delta^{*}_{1}}{\delta}}\cdot\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(y)}{1-{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(y)}}=1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\frac{\delta^{*}_{1}}{\delta}}\left(\alpha\cdot\frac{m_{2}(y)}{m_{0}(y)}+(1-\alpha)\right)+\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{\delta^{*}_{1}}{\delta}}\cdot\right)}.

(C.19)

We have found a candidate function
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}\colon{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}
that satisfies both
Equations

C.10
and
C.11
.
It remains to verify that this function meets
Item

(A7)
.
In
Section

C.2
(see
Equations

C.45
,
C.48
and
C.46
), we will show that
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}
satisfies

\displaystyle 0<\sup_{y\in\supp(Y)}{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(y)<1,\quad\inf_{y\in\supp(Y)}{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(y)>-\infty.

(C.20)

Since
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(y)<1
for every
y\in\supp(Y)
, writing

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathcal{T}}}_{1}^{*}(y)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{\frac{\sqrt{\delta_{1}^{*}/\delta}}{1-{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(y)}+1-\sqrt{\delta_{1}^{*}/\delta}},

we see that
\widetilde{{\mathcal{T}}}_{1}^{*}(y)
increases as
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(y)
increases.
Therefore,
Equation

C.20
implies

\displaystyle 0<\sup_{y\in\supp(Y)}\widetilde{{\mathcal{T}}}_{1}^{*}(y)<1,\quad\inf_{y\in\supp(Y)}\widetilde{{\mathcal{T}}}_{1}^{*}(y)>1-\frac{1}{1-\sqrt{\delta_{1}^{*}/\delta}},

(C.21)

which certifies that
\widetilde{{\mathcal{T}}}_{1}^{*}
satisfies
Item

(A7)
.

C.2
Optimization of spectral overlap

We will now identify the optimal preprocessing function that achieves the largest asymptotic overlap with
x_{1}^{*}
whenever
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
.
We again only focus on
x_{1}^{*}
, as the argument for
x_{2}^{*}
is analogous.
Recall from
Corollary

3.5
and
Lemma

I.4
that the squared overlap induced by a generic preprocessing function converges almost surely as:

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\left\langle v_{1}(D),x_{1}^{*}\right\rangle^{2}}{\left\|v_{1}(D)^{2}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|x_{1}^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha}\cdot\frac{\psi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1});\delta)}{\psi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1});\delta_{1})-\varphi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1}))}=\frac{1}{\alpha}\cdot\frac{\frac{1}{\delta}-\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-Z}\right)^{2}\right]}{\frac{1}{\delta_{1}}+\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-Z}\right)^{2}(G^{2}-1)\right]}

almost surely. Let
{\mathcal{F}}
denote the set of feasible preprocessing functions:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{{\mathcal{T}}\colon{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}:\inf_{y\in\supp(Y)}{\mathcal{T}}(y)>-\infty,\;0<\sup_{y\in\supp(Y)}{\mathcal{T}}(y)<\infty,\;\Pr\left[{\mathcal{T}}(Y)=0\right]<1\right\},

(C.22)

where
Y=q(G,\varepsilon)
and
(G,\varepsilon)\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\otimes P_{\varepsilon}
.
The variational problem we would like to solve is

\displaystyle\mathsf{OL}_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sup_{{\mathcal{T}}_{1}\in{\mathcal{F}}}\,\frac{1}{\alpha}\cdot\frac{\psi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1});\delta)}{\psi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1});\delta_{1})-\varphi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1}))}

\displaystyle\quad\mathrm{\ s.t.}\quad\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1});\delta_{1})=\varphi(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha}\sup_{{\mathcal{T}}_{1}\in{\mathcal{F}}}\frac{\psi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1});\delta)}{\psi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1});\delta_{1})-\varphi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1}))}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\mathrm{\ s.t.}\quad\psi(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1});\delta_{1})=\varphi(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})),\quad\psi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1});\delta)>0

(C.23)

where
\mathsf{OL}_{1}^{2}
is the squared overlap for the first signal.
The first constraint in
Equation

C.23
is by the definition of
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})
and the second one is by an equivalent characterization of the spectral threshold (cf.
Item

4
in
Lemma

I.3
).
We claim that
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})
can be assumed without loss of generality to be
1
.
To see this, according to the explicit formulas for
\varphi^{\prime}(\cdot)
,
\psi^{\prime}(\cdot;\delta_{1})
, and
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})
(see
Equations

I.1
,
I.2
and
I.4
), we note that both the objective and constraints of the optimization problem
\mathsf{OL}_{1}^{2}
depend on
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}\colon{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}
only through the ratio
\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(Y)}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(Y)}
.
Therefore, any
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}
and the corresponding
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})
computed via
Equation

I.2
can be replaced without affecting anything with
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}/\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})
and
1
, respectively.
Recall that
\Gamma_{1}\colon{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}
is defined as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}(y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(y)}{1-{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(y)}=\frac{1}{1-{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(y)}-1.

(C.24)

Since
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})=1
and
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})
is the solution to
Equation

I.4
in the interval
(\sup\supp(Z),\infty)
,
we need an additional constraint on
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}
:

\displaystyle 1>\sup\supp({\mathcal{T}}_{1}(Y))=\sup_{y\in\supp(Y)}{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(y),

(C.25)

which, in light of
Equation

C.24
, translates to the following constraint on
\Gamma_{1}
:

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)

\displaystyle>-1.

(C.26)

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be the feasible set of
\Gamma_{1}\colon{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}
.
To give a precise definition of
{\mathcal{G}}
, we now translate the constraints on
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}
to constraints on
\Gamma_{1}
.
According to
Equation

C.25
and the second equality in
Equation

C.24
,
\Gamma_{1}(y)
increases as
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(y)
increases in
(-\infty,1)
.
Therefore, the constraints
\sup\limits_{y\in\supp(Y)}{\mathcal{T}}(y)\in(0,\infty)
and
\inf\limits_{y\in\supp(Y)}{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(y)>-\infty
translate to

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)

\displaystyle\in(0,\infty),\qquad\inf_{y\in\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)>-1.

(C.27)

Equations

C.27
and
C.26
yield the following definition of
{\mathcal{G}}
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\Gamma\colon{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}:\inf_{y\in\supp(Y)}\Gamma(y)>-1,\;0<\sup_{y\in\supp(Y)}\Gamma(y)<\infty,\;\Pr\left[\Gamma(Y)=0\right]<1\right\}.

(C.28)

Using the explicit representations of various functionals and variables, we can then write the optimization problem
\mathsf{OL}_{1}^{2}
as

\displaystyle\mathsf{OL}_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sup_{{\mathcal{T}}_{1}\in{\mathcal{F}}}\frac{\frac{1}{\delta}-\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-Z}\right)^{2}\right]}{\frac{1}{\delta}+\alpha\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-Z}\right)^{2}(G^{2}-1)\right]}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\Gamma_{1}\in{\mathcal{G}}}\left(\frac{\frac{1}{\delta}+\alpha\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)^{2}m_{2}(y)\mathrm{d}y-\alpha\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)^{2}m_{0}(y)\mathrm{d}y}{\frac{1}{\delta}-\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)^{2}m_{0}(y)\mathrm{d}y}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\Gamma_{1}\in{\mathcal{G}}}\left(\frac{\frac{1-\alpha}{\delta}+\alpha\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)^{2}m_{2}(y)\mathrm{d}y}{\frac{1}{\delta}-\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)^{2}m_{0}(y)\mathrm{d}y}+\alpha\right)^{-1},

subject to the conditions

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-Z}(G^{2}-1)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha\delta},\qquad\frac{1}{\delta}>\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})-Z}\right)^{2}\right],

(C.29)

which can be alternatively written as follows using the notation introduced in
Section

C.1
:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha\delta}

\displaystyle=\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)(m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y))\mathrm{d}y,\qquad\frac{1}{\delta}>\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)^{2}m_{0}(y)\mathrm{d}y.

(C.30)

Note that in the first identity in
Equation

C.29
, we use
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})\neq 0
which holds since
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1})>\sup\supp(Z)>0
by
Item

(A7)
.
We observe that to solve
\mathsf{OL}_{1}^{2}
, it suffices to solve the following minimization problem:

\displaystyle\mathsf{OPT}_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf_{\Gamma_{1}\in{\mathcal{G}}}\,\left(\frac{\frac{1-\alpha}{\delta}+\alpha\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)^{2}m_{2}(y)\mathrm{d}y}{\frac{1}{\delta}-\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)^{2}m_{0}(y)\mathrm{d}y}\right)

\displaystyle\phantom{\coloneqq}\mathrm{\ s.t.}\quad\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{eqn:cond-opt-overlap}}.

Recall the standard fact that the minimum value of a function (subject to constraints) is the smallest level
\beta
such that the
\beta
-level set (after taking the intersection with the constraint set) is non-empty.
For any given
\beta>0
, define
{\mathcal{L}}_{\beta}
as the set of
\Gamma_{1}\in{\mathcal{G}}
which induces an objective value at most
\beta
and satisfies all conditions of
\mathsf{OPT}_{1}
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\beta}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\Gamma_{1}\in{\mathcal{G}}:\begin{array}[]{l}\frac{\frac{1-\alpha}{\delta}+\alpha\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)^{2}m_{2}(y)\mathrm{d}y}{\frac{1}{\delta}-\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)^{2}m_{0}(y)\mathrm{d}y}\leq\beta\\
\frac{1}{\alpha\delta}=\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)(m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y))\mathrm{d}y\\
\frac{1}{\delta}>\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)^{2}m_{0}(y)\mathrm{d}y\end{array}\right\}.

The first constraint describes the
\beta
-level set of the original objective of
\mathsf{OPT}_{1}
.
Then
\mathsf{OPT}_{1}
can be further written as

\displaystyle\mathsf{OPT}_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf\left\{\beta>0:{\mathcal{L}}_{\beta}\neq\emptyset\right\}.

(C.34)

The first constraint in
{\mathcal{L}}_{\beta}
is equivalent to:

\displaystyle\frac{1-\alpha}{\delta}+\alpha\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)^{2}m_{2}(y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\beta}{\delta}-\beta\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)^{2}m_{0}(y)\mathrm{d}y,

or

\displaystyle\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)^{2}(\alpha m_{2}(y)+\beta m_{0}(y))\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\beta-(1-\alpha)}{\delta},

(C.35)

since the third condition guarantees that the denominator of the LHS of the first inequality is positive.
We also claim that
\beta-(1-\alpha)>0
and divide both sides by it to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)^{2}\frac{\alpha m_{2}(y)+\beta m_{0}(y)}{\beta-(1-\alpha)}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\delta}.

(C.36)

To see why
\beta-(1-\alpha)>0
, recall that
\beta
is a possible value of
\mathsf{OPT}_{1}
which in turn has the following relation to
\mathsf{OL}_{1}^{2}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{OL}_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathsf{OPT}_{1}+\alpha}.

(C.37)

Since
0\leq\mathsf{OL}_{1}^{2}\leq 1
, this implies
\beta\geq 1-\alpha
.
Furthermore, if
\beta=1-\alpha
,
Equation

C.35
implies that
\Gamma_{1}(y)=0
for almost every
y\in\supp(Y)
.
However, since
\Gamma_{1}\in{\mathcal{G}}
, this cannot happen according to the third condition in the definition of
{\mathcal{G}}
(cf.
Equation

C.28
).
Therefore
\beta>1-\alpha
.

The following observation can further simplify the description of the set
{\mathcal{L}}_{\beta}
.
The third (inequality) constraint can be dropped since
m_{2}(y),m_{0}(y)\geq 0
and

\displaystyle\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)^{2}\frac{\alpha m_{2}(y)+\beta m_{0}(y)}{\beta-(1-\alpha)}\mathrm{d}y\geq\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)^{2}m_{0}(y)\mathrm{d}y.

Therefore the third constraint has already been guaranteed to be true given the first (inequality) constraint which is, as we just argued, equivalent to
Equation

C.36
.

Now, with the above observations, we arrive at the following equivalent description of the
\beta
-level set
{\mathcal{L}}_{\beta}
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\left\{\Gamma_{1}\in{\mathcal{G}}:\begin{array}[]{l}\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)^{2}\frac{\alpha m_{2}(y)+\beta m_{0}(y)}{\beta-(1-\alpha)}\mathrm{d}y\leq\frac{1}{\delta}\\
\frac{1}{\alpha\delta}=\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)(m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y))\mathrm{d}y\end{array}\right\}.

We observe that for any fixed
\beta>0
,
{\mathcal{L}}_{\beta}\neq\emptyset
if and only if
\mathsf{INF}_{1}^{(\beta)}\leq\frac{1}{\delta}
where
\mathsf{INF}_{1}^{(\beta)}
is the value of the following constrained minimization problem:

\displaystyle\mathsf{INF}_{1}^{(\beta)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf_{\Gamma_{1}\in{\mathcal{G}}}\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)^{2}\frac{\alpha m_{2}(y)+\beta m_{0}(y)}{\beta-(1-\alpha)}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\phantom{\coloneqq}\mathrm{\ s.t.}\quad\frac{1}{\alpha\delta}=\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}(y)(m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y))\mathrm{d}y.

We turn to compute the value of
\mathsf{INF}_{1}^{(\beta)}
.
Though
\mathsf{INF}_{1}^{(\beta)}
appears as a constrained variational problem over function space, one can obtain its optimum (and the corresponding optimizer) from a different perspective by casting it as a
linear program
over a certain Hilbert space.
Specifically, motivated by the form of the objective of
\mathsf{INF}_{1}^{(\beta)}
, we define the following inner product on the function space:

\displaystyle\left\langle f,g\right\rangle_{\beta,\alpha}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\supp(Y)}f(y)g(y)\frac{\alpha m_{2}(y)+\beta m_{0}(y)}{\beta-(1-\alpha)}\mathrm{d}y.

This induces the norm
\left\|f\right\|_{\beta,\alpha}=\sqrt{\left\langle f,f\right\rangle_{\beta,\alpha}}

With the above notation,
\mathsf{INF}_{1}^{(\beta)}
can be written as

\displaystyle\mathsf{INF}_{1}^{(\beta)}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\Gamma_{1}\in{\mathcal{G}}}\left\|\Gamma_{1}\right\|_{\beta,\alpha}^{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\mathrm{\ s.t.}\quad\left\langle\Gamma_{1},\frac{m_{2}-m_{0}}{\frac{\alpha m_{2}+\beta m_{0}}{\beta-(1-\alpha)}}\right\rangle_{\beta,\alpha}=\frac{1}{\alpha\delta}.

In words,
\mathsf{INF}_{1}^{(\beta)}
outputs the smallest norm of
\Gamma_{1}
whose linear projection onto a given vector (viewed as an element in the defined Hilbert space)

\frac{m_{2}-m_{0}}{\frac{\alpha m_{2}+\beta m_{0}}{\beta-(1-\alpha)}}
is fixed.
It is now geometrically clear that the minimizer
\Gamma_{1}^{(\beta)}
must be aligned with the vector
\frac{m_{2}-m_{0}}{\frac{\alpha m_{2}+\beta m_{0}}{\beta-(1-\alpha)}}
.
Therefore
\Gamma_{1}^{(\beta)}=a^{*}\cdot\frac{m_{2}-m_{0}}{\frac{\alpha m_{2}+\beta m_{0}}{\beta-(1-\alpha)}}
,
where the scalar
a^{*}\in{\mathbb R}
is uniquely determined from the equality condition:

\displaystyle\int_{\supp(Y)}a^{*}\cdot\frac{m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y)}{\frac{\alpha m_{2}(y)+\beta m_{0}(y)}{\beta-(1-\alpha)}}\cdot(m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y))\mathrm{d}y=\frac{1}{\alpha\delta},

i.e.,

\displaystyle a^{*}

\displaystyle=\left(\alpha\delta(\beta-(1-\alpha))\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{(m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y))^{2}}{\alpha m_{2}(y)+\beta m_{0}(y)}\mathrm{d}y\right)^{-1}.

Let

\displaystyle f_{\alpha}(\beta)

\displaystyle\coloneqq(\beta-(1-\alpha))\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{(m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y))^{2}}{\alpha m_{2}(y)+\beta m_{0}(y)}\mathrm{d}y.

We have found the minimizer of
\mathsf{INF}_{1}^{(\beta)}
:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}^{(\beta)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha\delta f_{\alpha}(\beta)}(\beta-(1-\alpha))\frac{m_{2}-m_{0}}{\alpha m_{2}+\beta m_{0}},

and the resulting minimum value is given by

\displaystyle\mathsf{INF}_{1}^{(\beta)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\supp(Y)}\Gamma_{1}^{(\beta)}(y)^{2}\frac{\alpha m_{2}(y)+\beta m_{0}(y)}{\beta-(1-\alpha)}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\supp(Y)}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha\delta f_{\alpha}(\beta)}(\beta-(1-\alpha))\frac{m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y)}{\alpha m_{2}(y)+\beta m_{0}(y)}\right)^{2}\frac{\alpha m_{2}(y)+\beta m_{0}(y)}{\beta-(1-\alpha)}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\alpha\delta f_{\alpha}(\beta))^{2}}\int_{\supp(Y)}(\beta-(1-\alpha))\frac{(m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y))^{2}}{\alpha m_{2}(y)+\beta m_{0}(y)}\mathrm{d}y\,=\,\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}\delta^{2}f_{\alpha}(\beta)}.

It follows that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\beta}\neq\emptyset

\displaystyle\quad\iff\quad\mathsf{INF}_{1}^{(\beta)}\leq\frac{1}{\delta}\quad\iff\quad\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}\delta^{2}f_{\alpha}(\beta)}\leq\frac{1}{\delta}\quad\iff\quad f_{\alpha}(\beta)\geq\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}\delta}.

Recalling
Equation

C.34
, the value of
\mathsf{OPT}_{1}
is therefore equal to

\displaystyle\mathsf{OPT}_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf\left\{\beta>0:f_{\alpha}(\beta)\geq\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}\delta}\right\}.

Writing
f_{\alpha}
as

\displaystyle f_{\alpha}(\beta)

\displaystyle=(\beta-(1-\alpha))\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{(m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y))^{2}}{\alpha m_{2}(y)+\beta m_{0}(y)}\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{(m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y))^{2}}{\frac{\alpha m_{2}(y)}{\beta-(1-\alpha)}+\frac{m_{0}(y)}{1-\frac{1-\alpha}{\beta}}}\mathrm{d}y,

we see that
f_{\alpha}
is increasing in
\beta
.
This implies that
\mathsf{OPT}_{1}
is equal to the critical
\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)
that solves the following equation

\displaystyle f_{\alpha}(\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}\delta}.

(C.40)

Putting our findings together, we have shown that the value of
\mathsf{OPT}_{1}
equals
\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)
satisfying
Equation

C.40
, or more explicitly,

\displaystyle(\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)-(1-\alpha))\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{(m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y))^{2}}{\alpha m_{2}(y)+\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)m_{0}(y)}\mathrm{d}y=\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}\delta},

(C.41)

and the corresponding optimizer is given by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}^{*}=\Gamma_{1}^{(\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha))}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha\delta f_{\alpha}(\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha))}(\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)-(1-\alpha))\frac{m_{2}-m_{0}}{\alpha m_{2}+\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)m_{0}}

\displaystyle=\alpha(\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)-(1-\alpha))\frac{m_{2}-m_{0}}{\alpha m_{2}+\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)m_{0}},

(C.42)

where the second equality follows from
Equation

C.41
.

In light of the relation between
\mathsf{OL}_{1}^{2}
and
\mathsf{OPT}_{1}
(cf.
Equation

C.37
) and the relation between
\Gamma_{1}
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}
(cf.
Equation

C.24
), it is straightforward to translate results in
Equations

C.41
and
C.42
to the original problem
\mathsf{OL}_{1}^{2}
we are interested in.
Indeed, the value of
\mathsf{OL}_{1}^{2}
equals

\displaystyle\mathsf{OL}_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)+\alpha}

(C.43)

and is achieved by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma_{1}^{*}}{1+\Gamma_{1}^{*}}=\frac{\alpha(\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)-(1-\alpha))\frac{m_{2}-m_{0}}{\alpha m_{2}+\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)m_{0}}}{1+\alpha(\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)-(1-\alpha))\frac{m_{2}-m_{0}}{\alpha m_{2}+\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)m_{0}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha(\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)-(1-\alpha))(m_{2}-m_{0})}{\alpha(\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)+\alpha)m_{2}+(1-\alpha)(\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)+\alpha)m_{0}}.

Recall that a multiplicative scaling of
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}
does not change its performance in terms of spectral threshold and overlap.
Therefore, we multiply the above expression by
\frac{\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)+\alpha}{\beta_{1}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)-(1-\alpha)}
and redefine
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}
as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{2}-m_{0}}{m_{2}+\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}m_{0}}=1-\frac{1}{\alpha\cdot\frac{m_{2}}{m_{0}}+(1-\alpha)}.

(C.44)

We observe that
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}
in
Equation

C.44
above is the same as that in
Equation

C.18
obtained in
Section

C.1
. This is discussed in
Remark

C.1
below.

Finally, we claim that the supremum in
\mathsf{OL}_{1}^{2}
can be
achieved
, by verifying that
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}
meets
Item

(A7)
.
Indeed, letting
(G,\varepsilon)\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\otimes P_{\varepsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle\inf_{y\in\supp({\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(q(G,\varepsilon)))}{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(y)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{1}{1-\alpha}=-\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}>-\infty,

(C.45)

provided
\alpha<1
.
Also, it trivially holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in\supp({\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(q(G,\varepsilon)))}{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(y)

\displaystyle<1.

(C.46)

We then verify
\Pr\left[{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(q(G,\varepsilon))=0\right]<1
.
To this end, observe that
m_{2}
cannot be identically equal to
m_{0}
, otherwise
\delta_{1}^{*}=\delta_{2}^{*}=\infty
(cf.
Equation

C.15
).
Therefore
m_{2}/m_{0}
is not constantly
1
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}
is not constantly
0
.
Finally, we verify that

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in\supp({\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(q(G,\varepsilon)))}\frac{m_{2}(y)}{m_{0}(y)}

\displaystyle>1,

(C.47)

which implies

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in\supp({\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(q(G,\varepsilon)))}{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(y)

\displaystyle>1-\frac{1}{\alpha+(1-\alpha)}=0.

(C.48)

Equation

C.47
follows since
m_{2}\not\equiv m_{0}
and by
Equation

D.2
,

\displaystyle\int_{\sup({\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(q(G,\varepsilon)))}(m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y))\mathrm{d}y=0,

hence there must exist
y\in\sup({\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(q(G,\varepsilon)))
such that
m_{2}(y)>m_{0}(y)
.

Remark C.1

(Coincidence of
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}
in
Sections

C.1
and
C.2
)
.

As noted in the proofs,
Equations

C.18
and
C.44
coincide.
The first part of
Section

C.1
exhibits a lower bound
\delta_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\geq\delta_{1}^{*}
(defined in
Equation

C.9
), whereas the second part (from
Equation

C.10
onward) shows
\delta_{1}^{\mathrm{spec}}\leq\delta_{1}^{*}
.
The upper bound can be alternatively obtained in
Section

C.2
by substituting in the condition
\psi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{1});\delta)>0
(cf.
Equation

C.23
) the function
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}
and recognizing that the condition is equivalent to
\delta>\delta_{1}^{*}
.
This recognition is, however, not entirely obvious and we find it more transparent to directly derive the upper bound in the second part of
Section

C.1
.
The price is that a slightly tilted version of
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}
(see
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}
in
Equation

C.19
) is constructed.
The tilting is an artifact of the proof technique and we expect that
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}
simultaneously minimizes the spectral threshold and maximizes the limiting overlap above the threshold.

Appendix D
Universal lower bound on spectral threshold (missing proof in
Remark

3.8
)

We show that for
i\in\{1,2\}
, we have
\delta^{\mathrm{spec}}_{i}\geq\frac{1}{2\alpha_{i}^{2}}
for
any
mixed GLM.
This follows from the upper bound

\displaystyle\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{(m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y))^{2}}{m_{0}(y)}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq 2,

(D.1)

in view of
Equation

3.20
. Here the functions
m_{2}
and
m_{0}
are defined in
Equation

C.14
.

A similar argument has been made in
[
LL20
, Appendix A]
for the non-mixed
complex
generalized linear model where the output
y_{i}
depends on the linear measurement
g_{i}=\left\langle a_{i},x^{*}\right\rangle
through its
modulus
:
y_{i}\sim p(\cdot\,|\,|g_{i}|)
.
Here we provide a similar argument for the real case without requiring the modulus.
Recalling the definition of
m_{2}
and
m_{0}
(cf.
Equation

C.14
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{(m_{2}(y)-m_{0}(y))^{2}}{m_{0}(y)}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{m_{2}(y)^{2}}{m_{0}(y)}\mathrm{d}y-2\int_{\supp(Y)}m_{2}(y)\mathrm{d}y+\int_{\supp(Y)}m_{0}(y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{m_{2}(y)^{2}}{m_{0}(y)}\mathrm{d}y-1,

since

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\supp(Y)}m_{2}(y)\mathrm{d}y&=\int_{\supp(Y)}\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)G^{2}\right]\mathrm{d}y=\mathbb{E}\left[G^{2}\right]=1,\\
\int_{\supp(Y)}m_{0}(y)\mathrm{d}y&=\int_{\supp(Y)}\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]\mathrm{d}y=1.\end{split}

(D.2)

To show
Equation

D.1
, it then suffices to show
\int\frac{m_{2}^{2}}{m_{0}}\leq 3
.
Using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we can bound
m_{2}^{2}
as follows:

\displaystyle m_{2}(y)^{2}=\left(\int_{\mathbb R}f(g)p(y|g)g^{2}\mathrm{d}g\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathbb R}\sqrt{f(g)p(y|g)}g^{2}\cdot\sqrt{f(g)p(y|g)}\mathrm{d}g\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb R}f(g)p(y|g)g^{4}\mathrm{d}g\right)\cdot\left(\int_{\mathbb R}f(g)p(y|g)\mathrm{d}g\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathbb R}f(g)p(y|g)g^{4}\mathrm{d}g\right)\cdot m_{0}(y),

where
f(g)
denotes the standard Gaussian density function.
The desired bound then follows:

\displaystyle\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{m_{2}(y)^{2}}{m_{0}(y)}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\supp(Y)}\int_{\mathbb R}f(g)p(y|g)g^{4}\mathrm{d}g\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb R}\left[f(g)\left(\int_{\supp(Y)}p(y|g)\mathrm{d}y\right)g^{4}\right]\mathrm{d}g=\mathbb{E}\left[G^{4}\right]=3.

Appendix E
Evaluation of spectral threshold and overlap

In this section, we evaluate the formulas in
Proposition

3.6
for mixed linear regression and mixed phase retrieval. Recall that the optimal spectral threshold for weak recovery of
x_{i}^{*}
is:

\displaystyle\delta^{*}_{i}

\displaystyle=\left(\alpha_{i}^{2}\int_{\supp(Y)}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)(G^{2}-1)\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]}\mathrm{d}y\right)^{-1},\qquad i\in\{1,2\}.

(E.1)

E.1
Mixed linear regression (proof of
Corollary

3.8
)

For the mixed linear regression model in
Equation

3.26
,

p(y|g)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{(y-g)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)
.
We first evaluate the expectations within the integral in
Equation

E.1
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]=\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{g^{2}}{2}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{(y-g)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)\mathrm{d}g=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(1+\sigma^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2(1+\sigma^{2})}\right),

(E.2)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)G^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{g^{2}}{2}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{(y-g)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)g^{2}\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(1+\sigma^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2(1+\sigma^{2})}\right)\cdot\frac{y^{2}+\sigma^{2}+\sigma^{4}}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}.

(E.3)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)(G^{2}-1)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(1+\sigma^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2(1+\sigma^{2})}\right)\cdot\frac{y^{2}-(1+\sigma^{2})}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}},

(E.4)

and

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)(G^{2}-1)\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{y^{2}-(1+\sigma^{2})}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}\right)^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(1+\sigma^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2(1+\sigma^{2})}\right).

The evaluation of integral in
Equation

E.1
can then be reduced to computing Gaussian moments:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)(G^{2}-1)\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb R}\left(\frac{y^{2}-(1+\sigma^{2})}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}\right)^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(1+\sigma^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2(1+\sigma^{2})}\right)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{Y\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1+\sigma^{2})}\left[\left(\frac{Y^{2}-(1+\sigma^{2})}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}\right)^{2}\right]=\frac{2}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}.

This yields
\delta^{*}_{i}=\frac{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}{2\alpha_{i}^{2}}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Furthermore, according to
Equation

3.23
, the corresponding optimal preprocessing functions specialize to

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{i}^{*}(y)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}\cdot\frac{y^{2}+\sigma^{2}+\sigma^{4}}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}+(1-\alpha_{i})}.

(E.5)

From
Equation

3.21
, the optimal overlap with signal
x^{*}_{i}
is
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\beta_{i}^{*}(\delta,\alpha,\sigma)+\alpha_{i}}}
. Using
the formulas in
Equations

E.2
,
E.3
and
E.4
, the fixed point equation
Equation

3.22
to determine
\beta_{i}^{*}(\delta,\alpha,\sigma)
can be simplified to

\displaystyle(\beta_{i}^{*}(\delta,\alpha,\sigma)-(1-\alpha_{i}))\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)}\left[\frac{\left(\frac{G^{2}-1}{1+\sigma^{2}}\right)^{2}}{\alpha_{i}\cdot\frac{G^{2}+\sigma^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}}+\beta_{i}^{*}(\delta,\alpha,\sigma)}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}^{2}\delta}\,,\quad i\in\{1,2\}.

(E.6)

E.2
Mixed phase retrieval (proof of
Corollary

3.9
)

For the mixed phase retrieval model in
Equation

3.27
,
p(y|g)\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{(y-|g|)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)
.
We first evaluate
\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]
and
\mathbb{E}\left[p(|G)G^{2}\right]
as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{g^{2}}{2}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{(y-|g|)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(1+\sigma^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2(1+\sigma^{2})}\right)\left[1+\mathrm{erf}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}(1+\sigma^{2})}}\right)\right],

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)G^{2}\right]=\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{g^{2}}{2}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{(y-|g|)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)g^{2}\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(1+\sigma^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2(1+\sigma^{2})}\right)\left[1+\mathrm{erf}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}(1+\sigma^{2})}}\right)\right]\frac{y^{2}+\sigma^{2}+\sigma^{4}}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\frac{\sigma y}{\pi(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right).

Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)(G^{2}-1)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(1+\sigma^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2(1+\sigma^{2})}\right)\left[1+\mathrm{erf}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}(1+\sigma^{2})}}\right)\right]\frac{y^{2}-(1+\sigma^{2})}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\frac{\sigma y}{\pi(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right),

and

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)(G^{2}-1)\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(1+\sigma^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2(1+\sigma^{2})}\right)\left[1+\mathrm{erf}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}(1+\sigma^{2})}}\right)\right]\cdot\left(\frac{y^{2}-(1+\sigma^{2})}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}\right)^{2}

(E.7)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+2\cdot\frac{y^{2}-(1+\sigma^{2})}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}\cdot\frac{\sigma y}{\pi(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)

(E.8)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\frac{\left[\frac{\sigma y}{\pi(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)\right]^{2}}{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(1+\sigma^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2(1+\sigma^{2})}\right)\left[1+\mathrm{erf}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}(1+\sigma^{2})}}\right)\right]}.

(E.9)

Let

\displaystyle\texttt{I}(y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{eqn:pr-eval-term1}},\quad\texttt{II}(y)\coloneqq\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{eqn:pr-eval-term2}},\quad\texttt{III}(y)\coloneqq\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{eqn:pr-eval-term3}}.

We will separately evaluate the integrals of
\texttt{I}(y),\texttt{II}(y)
, and
\texttt{III}(y)
.
The first two terms admit closed-form integrals:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb R}\texttt{I}(y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}},\quad\int_{\mathbb R}\texttt{II}(y)\mathrm{d}y=0.

For the third term, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb R}\texttt{III}(y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2}\sigma^{2}}{\pi^{3/2}(1+\sigma^{2})^{7/2}}\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{2+\sigma^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}(1+\sigma^{2})}y^{2}\right)y^{2}}{1+\mathrm{erf}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}(1+\sigma^{2})}}\right)}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{4\sigma^{5}}{\pi^{3/2}(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{\exp\left(-(2+\sigma^{2})z^{2}\right)z^{2}}{1+\mathrm{erf}(z)}\mathrm{d}z.

In the last equality, we apply the change of variable
z=\frac{y}{\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}(1+\sigma^{2})}}
.

Finally, defining

\displaystyle h(s)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{\exp\left(-(2+s)z^{2}\right)z^{2}}{1+\mathrm{erf}(z)}\mathrm{d}z,

we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)(G^{2}-1)\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb R}\texttt{I}(y)\mathrm{d}y+\int_{\mathbb R}\texttt{II}(y)\mathrm{d}y+\int_{\mathbb R}\texttt{III}(y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}+\frac{4\sigma^{5}h(\sigma^{2})}{\pi^{3/2}(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}.

We conclude that for
i\in\{1,2\}
:

\displaystyle\delta^{*}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}^{2}}\left(\frac{2}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}+\frac{4\sigma^{5}h(\sigma^{2})}{\pi^{3/2}(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}\right)^{-1}.

(E.10)

Furthermore, using
Equation

3.23
, the corresponding optimal preprocessing functions are
{\mathcal{T}}_{i}^{*}(y)=1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}\Delta(y)+(1-\alpha_{i})}
,
where

\displaystyle\Delta(y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)G^{2}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]}\,=\,\frac{y^{2}+\sigma^{2}+\sigma^{4}}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}+\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\cdot\frac{\sigma y\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}(1+\sigma^{2})}\right)}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{3/2}\left[1+\mathrm{erf}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}(1+\sigma^{2})}}\right)\right]}.

As
\sigma\downarrow 0
, the asymptotic behaviours of
h(\sigma)
and
\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)(G^{2}-1)\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]}\mathrm{d}y
are as follows:

\displaystyle h(\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{e^{-2z^{2}}z^{2}}{1+\mathrm{erf}(z)}\cdot e^{-\sigma^{2}z^{2}}\mathrm{d}z\xrightarrow{\sigma\to 0}\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{e^{-2z^{2}}z^{2}}{1+\mathrm{erf}(z)}\mathrm{d}z\approx 1.22564\in(0,\infty).

Hence

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)(G^{2}-1)\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=2-4\sigma^{2}+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{4}),

and for
i\in\{1,2\}
,

\displaystyle\delta^{*}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+2\sigma^{2}}{2\alpha_{i}^{2}}+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{4}).

(E.11)

If
\sigma=0
,
\Delta(y)=y^{2}
, so the optimal preprocessing functions are
{\mathcal{T}}_{i}^{*}(y)=1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}y^{2}+(1-\alpha_{i})}
.
From
Equation

3.21
, the optimal overlap with signal
x^{*}_{i}
is
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\beta_{i}^{*}(\delta,\alpha,\sigma)+\alpha_{i}}}
.
The integral in
Equation

3.22
to determine
\beta_{i}^{*}(\delta,\alpha,\sigma)
can be evaluated explicitly:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)(G^{2}-1)\right]^{2}}{\alpha\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)G^{2}\right]+\beta\mathbb{E}\left[p(y|G)\right]}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb R}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2}\right)\frac{(y^{2}-1)^{2}}{\alpha y^{2}+\beta}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha+\beta}{\alpha^{2}}+\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2\alpha\beta}}\left(\frac{\alpha+\beta}{\alpha}\right)^{2}\exp\left(\frac{\beta}{2\alpha}\right)\mathrm{erfc}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{2\alpha}}\right).

Therefore the fixed point equations (
3.22
) can be written explicitly, for
i\in\{1,2\}
:

(\beta_{i}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)-(1-\alpha_{i}))\left[-\frac{\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)}{\alpha_{i}^{2}}+\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2\alpha_{i}\beta_{i}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)}}\left(\frac{\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)}{\alpha_{i}}\right)^{2}\right.\\
\left.\exp\left(\frac{\beta_{i}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)}{2\alpha_{i}}\right)\mathrm{erfc}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{i}^{*}(\delta,\alpha)}{2\alpha_{i}}}\right)\right]=\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}^{2}\delta}.

(E.12)

Appendix F
Random matrix and free probability theory background

For a finite positive Borel measure (including probability measures)
\mu
on
{\mathbb R}
, the
Stieltjes transform

s_{\mu}\colon{\mathbb C}\setminus\supp(\mu)\to{\mathbb C}
of
\mu
is defined as

\displaystyle s_{\mu}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{1}{x-z}\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

(F.1)

The
R
-transform

[
Voi86
]
of
\mu
is the convergent power series defined by

\displaystyle R_{\mu}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq s_{\mu}^{-1}(-z)-\frac{1}{z},

(F.2)

where
s_{\mu}^{-1}
is the functional inverse of
s_{\mu}
.
The
free additive convolution
, denoted by
\mu\boxplus\nu
, of two compactly supported probability measures
\mu
and
\nu
is a compactly supported probability measure characterized by the following relation:

\displaystyle R_{\mu\boxplus\nu}

\displaystyle=R_{\mu}+R_{\nu}.

(F.3)

For a symmetric matrix
M\in{\mathbb R}^{p\times p}
, the
empirical spectral distribution
of
M
is defined as

\displaystyle\mu_{M}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\delta_{\lambda_{i}(M)}.

Suppose
A,B\in{\mathbb R}^{p\times p}
are two (sequences of) deterministic matrices whose empirical spectral distributions converge weakly to two compactly supported distributions
\mu
and
\nu
, respectively.
Let
O
be a random orthogonal matrix distributed according to the Haar distribution on the orthogonal group
{\mathbb O}(p)
.
Then
A
and
OBO^{\top}
are almost surely
asymptotically free
.
The same conclusion continues to hold if
A
and
B
are random matrices independent of
O
.
As a result
[
Voi91
,
Spe93
]
, the empirical spectral distribution of
D\coloneqq A+OBO^{\top}
converges weakly to
\mu\boxplus\nu
almost surely.
The free additive convolution
\mu\boxplus\nu
is again a compactly supported distribution on
{\mathbb R}
.
We caution that the weak convergence of the empirical spectral distribution of
D
does
not
imply that all eigenvalues of
D
are close to
\supp(\mu\boxplus\nu)
.
The spectrum of
D
may contain outliers, though they must not affect the limiting spectral distribution
\mu\boxplus\nu
.

Given a pair of Borel probability measures
\mu
and
\nu
on
{\mathbb R}
, there exists a unique pair of analytic functions
w_{1},w_{2}\colon{\mathbb H}\to{\mathbb H}
satisfying the following three properties, where
{\mathbb H}\coloneqq\{z\in{\mathbb C}:\Im(z)>0\}
denotes the upper complex half-plane.

1.

For
j\in\{1,2\}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{z\to\infty}\frac{w_{j}(\iota z)}{\iota z}

\displaystyle=1,

where
\iota
denotes the imaginary unit.

2.

For every
z\in{\mathbb H}
,

\displaystyle s_{\mu\boxplus\nu}(z)

\displaystyle=s_{\mu}(w_{1}(z))=s_{\nu}(w_{2}(z))=\frac{1}{z-(w_{1}(z)+w_{2}(z))}.

(F.4)

3.

For any
z\in{\mathbb H}\cup{\mathbb R}
such that
w_{1}
is analytic at
z
,
w_{1}(z)
is the attracting fixed point of the self-map of
{\mathbb H}
defined by

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle\mapsto F_{\nu}(F_{\mu}(w)-w+z)-(F_{\mu}(w)-w)

where

\displaystyle F_{\mu}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\frac{1}{s_{\mu}(z)},\quad F_{\nu}(z)\coloneqq-\frac{1}{s_{\nu}(z)}.

A similar statement, with
\mu
and
\nu
interchanged, holds for
w_{2}
.

The functions
w_{1},w_{2}
defined through the above functional equations are called
subordination functions

[
Voi93
,
Bia98
,
BB07
]
.
We emphasize that each of the subordination functions
w_{1},w_{2}
depends on
both
distributions
\mu,\nu
.
Though
w_{1},w_{2}
are defined on the upper half-plane, they can be continuously extended to the real line.

Lemma F.1

(
[
BBCF17
, Lemma 3.1]
)
.

Let
\mu
and
\nu
be two compactly supported Borel probability measures on
{\mathbb R}
neither of which is a point mass.
Then the subordination functions
w_{1},w_{2}\colon{\mathbb H}\to{\mathbb H}
associated with
\mu,\nu
can be extended to
{\mathbb H}\cup{\mathbb R}\cup\{\infty\}
with the following properties being preserved.

1.

w_{1},w_{2}\colon{\mathbb H}\cup{\mathbb R}\cup\{\infty\}\to{\mathbb H}\cup{\mathbb R}\cup\{\infty\}
are continuous.

2.

If
x\in{\mathbb R}\setminus\supp(\mu\boxplus\nu)
, then
w_{1},w_{2}
continue meromorphically to a neighbourhood of
x
:

\displaystyle w_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=F_{\nu}(w_{2}(x))-w_{2}(x)+x\in({\mathbb R}\cup\{\infty\})\setminus\supp(\mu),

\displaystyle w_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=F_{\mu}(w_{1}(x))-w_{1}(x)+x\in({\mathbb R}\cup\{\infty\})\setminus\supp(\nu).

If
w_{1}(x)=\infty
, then

\displaystyle w_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=x-\int_{\mathbb R}z\mathrm{d}\mu(z)\in{\mathbb R},

and if
w_{2}(x)=\infty
, then

\displaystyle w_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=x-\int_{\mathbb R}z\mathrm{d}\nu(z)\in{\mathbb R}.

3.

Conversely, suppose that
w_{1}
continues meromorphically to a neighbourhood of
x\in{\mathbb R}
and that
w_{1}(z)\in{\mathbb R}
when
z\in(x-\delta,x+\delta)\setminus\{x\}
for some
\delta>0
.
If
x\in\supp(\mu\boxplus\nu)
, then
x
is an isolated atom for
\mu\boxplus\nu
.

Appendix G
Theorem 2.1 of
[
BBCF17
]

For the reader’s convenience, we transcribe below Theorem 2.1 in
[
BBCF17
]
.

G.1
Assumptions

Let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
be two compactly supported Borel probability measures on
{\mathbb R}
.
Let
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
be two positive integers.
Let
O\sim\haar({\mathbb O}(d))
be a (sequence of) random orthogonal matrix distributed according to the Haar distribution.
Let
\theta_{1}\geq\theta_{2}\geq\cdots\theta_{\ell_{1}}
and
\gamma_{1}\geq\gamma_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\gamma_{\ell_{2}}
be two fixed sequences of real numbers which do not depend on
\supp(\mu_{1})
and
\supp(\mu_{2})
, and denote
\Theta\coloneqq(\theta_{i})_{i=1}^{\ell_{1}},\Gamma=(\gamma_{i})_{i=1}^{\ell_{2}}
as the
multiset
of these two sequences of numbers, respectively.
Let
M=M_{1}+OM_{2}O^{\top}
where
M_{1}
,
M_{2}
and
O
are matrices satisfying the following assumptions.

1.

The matrix (sequence)
M_{1}\in{\mathbb R}^{d\times d}
is deterministic and symmetric for each
d\in{\mathbb Z}_{\geq 1}
and satisfies:

(a)

\mu_{M_{1}}\to\mu_{1}
weakly as
d\to\infty
;

(b)

for every
d\geq\ell_{1}
and
\theta\in\Theta
,

\displaystyle\left|\left\{i\in[d]:\lambda_{i}(M_{1})=\theta\right\}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left\{i\in[\ell_{1}]:\theta_{i}=\theta\right\}\right|.

In words, the number of eigenvalues of
M_{1}
that are equal to
\theta
equals the multiplicity of
\theta
in
\Theta
;

(c)

the set of eigenvalues of
M_{1}
that do not equal any
\theta\in\Theta
converges uniformly to
\supp(\mu_{1})
as
d\to\infty
, that is,

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[d]\\
\lambda_{i}(M_{1})\notin\Theta\end{subarray}}\dist(\lambda_{i}(M_{1}),\supp(\mu_{1}))

\displaystyle=0.

2.

The matrix (sequence)
M_{2}\in{\mathbb R}^{d\times d}
is deterministic and symmetric for each
d\in{\mathbb Z}_{\geq 1}
and satisfies:

(a)

\mu_{M_{2}}\to\mu_{2}
weakly as
d\to\infty
;

(b)

for every
d\geq\ell_{2}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
,

\displaystyle\left|\left\{i\in[d]:\lambda_{i}(M_{2})=\gamma\right\}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left\{i\in[\ell_{1}]:\gamma_{i}=\gamma\right\}\right|,

in words, the number of eigenvalues of
M_{2}
that are equal to
\gamma
equals the multiplicity of
\gamma
in
\Gamma
;

(c)

the set of eigenvalues of
M_{2}
that do not equal any
\gamma\in\Gamma
converges uniformly to
\supp(\mu_{2})
as
d\to\infty
, that is,

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[d]\\
\lambda_{i}(M_{2})\notin\Gamma\end{subarray}}\dist(\lambda_{i}(M_{2}),\supp(\mu_{2}))

\displaystyle=0.

Remark G.1
.

It was mentioned in
[
BBCF17
, Remark 2.7]
that the same results below (
Theorem

G.1
) hold if
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are random matrices such that
M_{1},M_{2},U
are mutually independent and the outlier eigenvalues of
M_{1}
(resp.
M_{2}
) converge to (instead of being exactly equal to)
\theta_{i}
(resp.
\gamma_{i}
) almost surely as
d\to\infty
.

Remark G.2
.

It was mentioned in
[
BBCF17
, Remark 2.8]
that analogous results below (
Theorem

G.1
) can be obtained if
M
is a sum of
k\geq 3
matrices.
Indeed, let
k\in{\mathbb Z}_{\geq 2}
,
\{A_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}\subset{\mathbb R}^{d\times d}
be deterministic symmetric matrices
4
4

4

Again, this condition can be relaxed so that
A_{i}
are independent random matrices with desired limiting spectral distribution and outlier eigenvalues. See
Remark

G.1
.
and
(U_{i})_{i\in[k-1]}\sim\haar({\mathbb O}(d))^{\otimes(k-1)}
be independent Haar matrices.
Suppose that for
i\in[k]
, the empirical spectral distribution of
A_{i}
converges weakly to
\mu_{i}
as
d\to\infty
and
A_{i}
has outlier eigenvalues subject to the assumptions in
Section

G.1
.
Let

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}U_{i}A_{i}U_{i}^{\top}+A_{k}.

Then the limiting spectral distribution of
M
equals
\bigboxplus_{i=1}^{k}\mu_{i}
, and the outlier eigenvalues of
M
can be described in an analogous manner as in
Theorem

G.1
below.

G.2
Theorem statement

Theorem G.1

(
[
BBCF17
, Theorem 2.1]
)
.

Let
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
be the subordination functions associated with the pair of probability measures
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
.
Define

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}\coloneqq\supp(\mu_{1}\boxplus\mu_{2}),\quad\widehat{\mathcal{K}}\coloneqq\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell_{1}}\{w_{1}^{-1}(\theta_{i})\}\cup\bigcup_{j=1}^{\ell_{2}}\{w_{2}^{-1}(\gamma_{j})\},\quad{\mathcal{K}}\coloneqq\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{K}}.

(G.1)

Then, the following statements hold.

1.

For any
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\exists d_{0},\,\forall d>d_{0},\,\forall i\in[d],\,\lambda_{i}(M)\in{\mathcal{K}}_{\varepsilon}\right]

\displaystyle=1,

where for a set of real numbers
{\mathcal{K}}\subset{\mathbb R}
, we use
{\mathcal{K}}_{\varepsilon}
to denote its
\varepsilon
-enlargement
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\left\{\rho\in{\mathbb R}:\inf_{\rho^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}}|\rho-\rho^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon\right\}.

In words, for all sufficiently large dimensions, all eigenvalues of
M
converge into
{\mathcal{K}}
.

2.

Fix some
\rho\in\widehat{\mathcal{K}}
, let
\varepsilon>0
be sufficiently small so that
(\rho-2\varepsilon,\rho+2\varepsilon)\cap\widehat{\mathcal{K}}=\{\rho\}
.
Set
k\coloneqq\left|\left\{i\in[\ell_{1}]:w_{1}(\rho)=\theta_{i}\right\}\right|
and
\ell=\left|\left\{i\in[\ell_{2}]:w_{2}(\rho)=\gamma_{i}\right\}\right|
.
That is,
k
and
\ell
are the numbers of the outliers of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, respectively, that go to the outlier
\rho
of
M
.
Then

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\exists d_{0},\,\forall d>d_{0},\,\left|\left\{i\in[d]:\lambda_{i}(M)\in(\rho-\varepsilon,\rho+\varepsilon)\right\}\right|=k+\ell\right]

\displaystyle=1.

In words, the multiplicity of the outlier eigenvalue
\rho
of
M
is
k+\ell
if there are
k
outliers of
M_{1}
and
\ell
outliers of
M_{2}
that correspond to
\rho
.

Appendix H
State evolution of GAMP for mixed GLMs (proof of
Proposition

6.1
)

Recall the GAMP iteration in
Equation

6.2
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}u^{t}&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}\overline{A}f_{t}(v^{t};\,\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*})-{\sf b}_{t}\,g_{t-1}(u^{t-1};y),\qquad v^{t+1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}\overline{A}^{\top}g_{t}(u^{t};y)-{\sf c}_{t}\,f_{t}(v^{t};\,\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*}),\end{split}

(H.1)

where the memory coefficients are
{\sf b}_{t}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{d}f_{t}^{\prime}(v_{i}^{t};\,\overline{x}_{1,i}^{*},\overline{x}_{2,i}^{*})
and

{\sf c}_{t}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{t}^{\prime}(u_{i}^{t};y_{i})
. Using the initialization
{\tilde{v}}^{0}
, the iteration starts with
u^{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}\overline{A}{\tilde{v}}^{0}
.

To prove the proposition, we rewrite
Equation

H.1
as an AMP iteration with matrix-valued iterates. This matrix-valued AMP is designed to be a special case of an abstract AMP iteration for which a state evolution result has been established in
[
JM13
,
FVRS22
]
. This state evolution result is then translated to obtain the results in
Equations

6.12
and
6.13
.
(See also
[
GB21
]
for an analysis of a general graph-based AMP iteration that includes AMP with matrix-valued iterates.)
Given the iteration in
Equation

H.1
, for
t\geq 1
, let

\displaystyle e^{t}\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}g_{1}&g_{2}&u^{t}\end{bmatrix},\qquad h^{t+1}\coloneqq\,

\displaystyle v^{t+1}-\chi_{1,t+1}\overline{x}_{1}^{*}-\chi_{2,t+1}\overline{x}_{2}^{*}\,,

(H.2)

where we recall that
g_{1}=\overline{A}\overline{x}_{1}^{*}
,
g_{2}=\overline{A}\overline{x}_{2}^{*}
, and
\chi_{1,t},\chi_{2,t}
are the state evolution parameters computed via the recursion in
Equations

6.4
,
6.5
and
6.6
.
We also introduce the functions
{\breve{f}}_{t}:{\mathbb R}^{3}\to{\mathbb R}^{3}
