and
{\breve{g}}_{t}:{\mathbb R}^{5}\to{\mathbb R}
, defined as:

\displaystyle{\breve{f}}_{t}(h^{t};\,\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*})=\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\delta}\overline{x}_{1}^{*},&\sqrt{\delta}\overline{x}_{2}^{*},&f_{t}(h^{t}+\chi_{1,t}\overline{x}_{1}^{*}+\chi_{2,t}\overline{x}_{2}^{*};\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*})\end{bmatrix},

(H.3)

\displaystyle{\breve{g}}_{t}(e^{t};\,{\underline{\eta}},{\underline{\varepsilon}})=g_{t}(e^{t}_{3};\,q({\underline{\eta}}\odot e^{t}_{1}+(1-{\underline{\eta}})\odot e^{t}_{2},{\underline{\varepsilon}})).

(H.4)

Here,
\odot
denotes element-wise multiplication,
{\breve{f}}_{t}
and
{\breve{g}}_{t}
act row-wise on their matrix-valued inputs and
e^{t}_{j}
denotes the
j
-th column of
e^{t}\in{\mathbb R}^{n\times 3}
. We also recall the notation
{\underline{\eta}}=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n})
,
{\underline{\varepsilon}}=(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n})
and that
y=q({\underline{\eta}}\odot g_{1}+(1-{\underline{\eta}})\odot g_{2},\,{\underline{\varepsilon}})=q({\underline{\eta}}\odot e^{t}_{1}+(1-{\underline{\eta}})\odot e^{t}_{2},\,{\underline{\varepsilon}})
. With these definitions, we claim that the AMP iteration in
Equation

H.1
is equivalent to the following one:

\begin{split}&e^{t}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}\overline{A}{\breve{f}}_{t}(h^{t};\,\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*})\,-\,{\breve{g}}_{t-1}(e^{t-1};\,{\underline{\eta}},{\underline{\varepsilon}}){\sf B}_{t}^{\top},\\
&h^{t+1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}\overline{A}^{\top}{\breve{g}}_{t}(e^{t};\,{\underline{\eta}},{\underline{\varepsilon}})\,-\,{\breve{f}}_{t}(h^{t};\,\overline{x}_{1}^{*},\overline{x}_{2}^{*}){\sf C}_{t}^{\top},\end{split}

(H.5)

where
{\sf B}_{t}\in{\mathbb R}^{3\times 1}
and
{\sf C}_{t}\in{\mathbb R}^{1\times 3}
are given by:

\displaystyle{\sf B}_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{d}f_{t}^{\prime}(h_{i}^{t}+\chi_{1,t}\overline{x}^{*}_{1,i}+\chi_{2,t}\overline{x}^{*}_{2,i};\overline{x}_{1,i}^{*},\overline{x}_{2,i}^{*})\end{bmatrix}^{\top},

\displaystyle{\sf C}_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}{\mathbb E}[\partial_{1}g_{t}(U_{t};\,q(\eta G_{1}+(1-\eta)G_{2},\varepsilon))]\\
{\mathbb E}[\partial_{2}g_{t}(U_{t};\,q(\eta G_{1}+(1-\eta)G_{2},\varepsilon))]\\
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{t}^{\prime}(u_{i}^{t};\,q(\eta_{i}g^{1}_{i}+(1-\eta_{i})g^{2}_{i},\varepsilon_{i}))]\end{bmatrix}^{\top}.

(H.6)

Here
\partial_{1}g_{t}
and
\partial_{2}g_{t}
refer to the partial derivatives of
g_{t}(u;\,q(\eta g_{1}+\eta g_{2},\varepsilon))
with respect to
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
, respectively,
(G_{1},G_{2},\eta,\varepsilon)\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\otimes\bern(\alpha)\otimes P_{\varepsilon}
, and
U_{t}
is defined as in
Equation

6.4
. The iteration in
Equation

H.5
is initialized with
e^{0}=[g_{1}\quad g_{2}\quad\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}\overline{A}{\tilde{v}}^{0}]
.

The equivalence between
Equations

H.1
and
H.5
can be seen by substituting in
Equation

H.5
the definitions of
e^{t}
and
h^{t+1}
from
Equation

H.2
, and the fact that by Stein’s lemma,
\chi_{1,t+1}
and
\chi_{2,t+1}
defined in
Equations

6.4
,
6.5
and
6.6
can be expressed as
[
FVRS22
, Lemma 4.1]
:

\chi_{1,t+1}=\sqrt{\delta}\,{\mathbb E}[\partial_{1}g_{t}(U_{t};q(\eta G_{1}+(1-\eta)G_{2},\varepsilon))],\;\chi_{2,t+1}=\sqrt{\delta}\,{\mathbb E}[\partial_{2}g_{t}(U_{t};q(\eta G_{1}+(1-\eta)G_{2},\varepsilon))].

(H.7)

The recursion in
Equation

H.5
is a special case of the abstract AMP recursion with matrix-valued iterates for which a state evolution result has been established
[
JM13
]
,
[
FVRS22
, Sec. 6.7]
. The standard form of the abstract AMP recursion uses empirical estimates (instead of expected values) for the first two entries of
{\sf C}_{t}
in
Equation

H.6
. However, the state evolution result remains valid for the recursion in
Equation

H.5
(see Remark 4.3 of
[
FVRS22
]
). This result states that the empirical distributions of the rows of
e^{t}
and
h^{t+1}
converge to the Gaussian distributions
{\mathcal{N}}(0,\Sigma_{t})
and
{\mathcal{N}}(0,\Omega_{t+1})
, respectively. The covariances
\Sigma_{t}\in{\mathbb R}^{3\times 3}
and
\Omega_{t+1}\in{\mathbb R}
are defined by the following state evolution recursion:

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta}{\mathbb E}[{\breve{f}}_{t}(G^{\omega}_{t};\,X_{1},X_{2}){\breve{f}}_{t}(G^{\omega}_{t};\,X_{1},X_{2})^{\top}]

(H.8)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&\frac{{\mathbb E}[X_{1}f_{t}(G^{\omega}_{t}+\chi_{1,t}X_{1}+\chi_{2,t}X_{2};X_{1},X_{2})]}{\sqrt{\delta}}\\
0&1&\frac{{\mathbb E}[X_{2}f_{t}(G^{\omega}_{t}+\chi_{1,t}X_{1}+\chi_{2,t}X_{2};X_{1},X_{2})]}{\sqrt{\delta}}\\
\frac{{\mathbb E}[X_{1}f_{t}(G^{\omega}_{t}+\chi_{1,t}X_{1}+\chi_{2,t}X_{2};X_{1},X_{2})]}{\sqrt{\delta}}&\frac{{\mathbb E}[X_{2}f_{t}(G^{\omega}_{t}+\chi_{1,t}X_{1}+\chi_{2,t}X_{2};X_{1},X_{2})]}{\sqrt{\delta}}&\frac{{\mathbb E}[(f_{t}(G^{\omega}_{t}+\chi_{1,t}X_{1}+\chi_{2,t}X_{2};X_{1},X_{2}))^{2}]}{\delta}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\Omega_{t+1}

\displaystyle={\mathbb E}[({\breve{g}}_{t}(G^{\sigma}_{t};\,\eta,\varepsilon))^{2}]={\mathbb E}[(g_{t}(G^{\sigma}_{t,3};\,q(G^{\sigma}_{t,1},G^{\sigma}_{t,2},\eta,\varepsilon)))^{2}],

(H.9)

where
G^{\sigma}_{t}\equiv(G^{\sigma}_{t,1},G^{\sigma}_{t,2},G^{\sigma}_{t,3})\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\Sigma_{t})
is independent of
(\eta,\varepsilon)\sim\bern(\alpha)\otimes P_{\varepsilon}
,
and
(G^{\omega}_{t},X_{1},X_{2})\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\Omega_{t})\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
. The recursion is initialized with

\Sigma_{0}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}{\mathbb E}[F_{0}(X_{1},X_{2})X_{1}]\\
0&1&\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}{\mathbb E}[F_{0}(X_{1},X_{2})X_{2}]\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}{\mathbb E}[F_{0}(X_{1},X_{2})X_{1}]&\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}{\mathbb E}[F_{0}(X_{1},X_{2})X_{2}]&\frac{1}{\delta}{\mathbb E}[(F_{0}(X_{1},X_{2}))^{2}]\end{bmatrix}.

(H.10)

The sequences
(G^{\sigma}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and

(G^{\omega}_{t+1})_{t\geq 0}
are each jointly Gaussian with the following covariance structure:

\begin{split}&G^{\sigma}_{t,1}=G_{1},\qquad G^{\sigma}_{t,2}=G_{2},\quad\forall\,t\geq 0\quad\text{ where }(G_{1},G_{2})\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0,1),\\
&\mathbb{E}\left[G^{\sigma}_{0,3}G^{\sigma}_{t,3}\right]=\frac{1}{\delta}\mathbb{E}\left[F_{0}(X_{1},X_{2})f_{t}(G^{\omega}_{t}+\chi_{1,t}X_{1}+\chi_{2,t}X_{2};X_{1},X_{2})\right],\quad t\geq 1,\end{split}

(H.11)

and for
r,t\geq 1
:

\mathbb{E}\left[G^{\omega}_{r}G^{\omega}_{t}\right]={\mathbb E}[g_{r-1}(G^{\sigma}_{r-1,3};\,q(\eta G^{\sigma}_{r-1,1}+(1-\eta)G^{\sigma}_{r-1,2},\varepsilon)\\
\times g_{t-1}(G^{\sigma}_{t-1,3};\,q(\eta G^{\sigma}_{t-1,1}+(1-\eta)G^{\sigma}_{t-1,2},\varepsilon)],

(H.12a)

\mathbb{E}\left[G^{\sigma}_{r,3}G^{\sigma}_{t,3}\right]=\frac{1}{\delta}\mathbb{E}\left[f_{r}(G^{\omega}_{r}+\chi_{1,r}X_{1}+\chi_{2,r}X_{2};X_{1},X_{2})\,f_{t}(G^{\omega}_{t}+\chi_{1,t}X_{1}+\chi_{2,t}X_{2};X_{1},X_{2})\right].

(H.12b)

The following proposition follows from the state evolution result in
[
JM13
]
,
[
FVRS22
, Sec. 6.7]
for AMP with matrix-valued iterates.

Proposition H.1

(State Evolution)
.

With the setup and assumptions of
Proposition

6.1
, consider the AMP recursion in
Equation

H.5
. The following holds almost surely for any
{\rm PL}(2)
functions
\Psi:{\mathbb R}^{t+1}\to{\mathbb R}
,
\Phi:{\mathbb R}^{t+3}\to{\mathbb R}
, for
t\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Psi(e^{t}_{i},e^{t-1}_{i},\ldots,e^{0}_{i})={\mathbb E}[\Psi(G^{\sigma}_{t},\,G^{\sigma}_{t-1},\,\ldots,G^{\sigma}_{0})],

(H.13)

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Phi(\overline{x}^{*}_{1,i},\overline{x}^{*}_{2,i},h^{t+1}_{i},h^{t}_{i},\ldots,h^{1}_{i})={\mathbb E}[\Phi(X_{1},X_{2},G^{\omega}_{t+1},\,G^{\omega}_{t},\,\ldots,G^{\omega}_{1})],

(H.14)

where the joint distributions of
(G^{\sigma}_{t},\,G^{\sigma}_{t-1},\,\ldots,G^{\sigma}_{0})
and
(G^{\omega}_{t+1},\,G^{\omega}_{t},\,\ldots,G^{\omega}_{1}))
are as given in
Equations

H.8
,
H.9
,
H.10
,
H.11
and
H.12b
.

Recalling the definitions of
e^{t},h^{t+1}
from
Equation

H.2
, the results in
Equations

H.13
and
H.14
imply that for any
{\rm PL}(2)
function
\Phi:{\mathbb R}^{t+3}\to{\mathbb R}
, we have for
t\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Phi(\overline{x}^{*}_{1,i},\overline{x}^{*}_{2,i},v^{t+1}_{i},\ldots,v^{1}_{i})

\displaystyle\qquad={\mathbb E}[\Phi(X_{1},X_{2},\chi_{1,t+1}X_{1}+\chi_{2,t+1}X_{2}+G^{\omega}_{t+1},\,\ldots,\,\chi_{1,1}X_{1}+\chi_{2,1}X_{2}+G^{\omega}_{1})]

(H.15)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi(g_{1,i},g_{2,i},u^{t}_{i},\ldots,u^{0}_{i})=\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi(G_{1},G_{2},G^{\sigma}_{t,3},\ldots,G^{\sigma}_{0,3})\right].

(H.16)

Recalling
G^{\sigma}_{t,1}=G_{1}
and
G^{\sigma}_{t,2}=G_{2}
for
t\geq 0
, using
Equation

H.8
we can write

\displaystyle G^{\sigma}_{t,3}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[G^{\sigma}_{t,3}\,|\,G^{\sigma}_{t,1},G^{\sigma}_{t,2}\right]\,+\,W^{\sigma}_{t}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}f_{t}(G^{\omega}_{t}+\chi_{1,t}X_{1}+\chi_{2,t}X_{2};X_{1},X_{2})\right]G_{1}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\,\hskip 140.00021pt+\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{2}f_{t}(G^{\omega}_{t}+\chi_{1,t}X_{1}+\chi_{2,t}X_{2};X_{1},X_{2})\right]G_{2}\,+\,W^{\sigma}_{t},

where
W^{\sigma}_{t}
is a zero-mean Gaussian independent of
(G_{1},G_{2})
with variance

\mathbb{E}\left[(W^{\sigma}_{t})^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{\delta}{\mathbb E}[(f_{t}(G^{\omega}_{t}+\chi_{1,t}X_{1}+\chi_{2,t}X_{2};X_{1},X_{2}))^{2}]\\
-\frac{1}{\delta}(\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}f_{t}(G^{\omega}_{t}+\chi_{1,t}X_{1}+\chi_{2,t}X_{2};X_{1},X_{2})\right])^{2}-\frac{1}{\delta}(\mathbb{E}\left[X_{2}f_{t}(G^{\omega}_{t}+\chi_{1,t}X_{1}+\chi_{2,t}X_{2};X_{1},X_{2})\right])^{2}.

(H.17)

To complete the proof, for
t\geq 0
, we define:

\begin{split}&W_{U,t}\coloneqq W^{\sigma}_{t},\quad W_{V,t+1}\coloneqq G^{\omega}_{t+1},\\
&\mu_{1,t}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}{\mathbb E}[X_{1}f_{t}(G^{\omega}_{t}+\chi_{1,t}X_{1}+\chi_{2,t}X_{2};X_{1},X_{2})],\quad\mu_{2,t}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}{\mathbb E}[X_{2}f_{t}(G^{\omega}_{t}+\chi_{1,t}X_{1}+\chi_{2,t}X_{2};X_{1},X_{2})],\\
&U_{t}\coloneqq G^{\sigma}_{3,t}=\mu_{1,t}G_{1}+\mu_{2,t}G_{2}+W_{U,t},\quad V_{t+1}=\chi_{1,t}X_{1}+\chi_{2,t}X_{2}+W_{V,t+1}.\end{split}

Using these in the convergence statements in
Equations

H.15
and
H.16
gives the result of
Proposition

6.1
.

Appendix I
Auxiliary lemmas

Lemma I.1
.

Consider the setting of
Section

2.4
and let
Item

(A7)
hold.
Then the following properties of
\varphi(\lambda),\psi(\lambda;\Delta)
hold.

1.

\varphi(\cdot)
is strictly decreasing;

2.

For any
\Delta>0
,
\psi(\cdot;\Delta)
is strictly convex in the first argument;

3.

For any
\lambda>\sup\supp(Z)
,
\psi(\lambda;\cdot)
is strictly decreasing in the second argument.

Proof.

The proof follows by checking the derivatives.
For
\varphi
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\varphi(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{ZG^{2}}{\lambda-Z}\right]-\lambda\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{ZG^{2}}{(\lambda-Z)^{2}}\right]=-\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{ZG}{\lambda-Z}\right)^{2}\right]<0,

(I.1)

The last strict inequality holds since
Z
is not almost surely zero, i.e.,
\Pr\left[Z=0\right]<1
in
Item

(A7)
.

Item

1
of
Lemma

I.1
then follows.
For
\psi
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\psi(\lambda;\Delta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Delta}+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{\lambda-Z}\right]-\lambda\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{(\lambda-Z)^{2}}\right]=\frac{1}{\Delta}-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z^{2}}{(\lambda-Z)^{2}}\right],

(I.2)

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\lambda^{2}}\psi(\lambda;\Delta)

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z^{2}}{(\lambda-Z)^{3}}\right].

Since
\lambda>\sup\supp(Z)
and
Z
is not almost surely zero,
\psi^{\prime\prime}(\cdot;\Delta)>0
and therefore
Item

2
of
Lemma

I.1
holds.
Finally,
Item

3
of
Lemma

I.1
is obvious, provided
\lambda>\sup\supp(Z)>0
where the second inequality is guaranteed by
Item

(A7)
.
∎

Lemma I.2

(Explicit formulas)
.

Fix any
\Delta>0
.
The parameter
\overline{\lambda}(\Delta)
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z}{\overline{\lambda}(\Delta)-Z}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Delta}.

(I.3)

If
\lambda^{*}(\Delta)>\overline{\lambda}(\Delta)
, the parameter
\lambda^{*}(\Delta)
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z(G^{2}-1)}{\lambda^{*}(\Delta)-Z}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Delta}.

(I.4)

Proof.

Since
\overline{\lambda}(\Delta)
(cf.
Equation

3.3
) is the minimum point of
\psi(\cdot;\Delta)
, it satisfies

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\psi(\lambda;\Delta)\right|_{\lambda=\overline{\lambda}(\Delta)}

\displaystyle=0.

This gives
Equation

I.3
according to
Equation

I.2
.

Under the condition
\lambda^{*}(\Delta)>\overline{\lambda}(\Delta)
, we have
\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\Delta);\Delta)=\psi(\lambda^{*}(\Delta);\Delta)
and
\lambda^{*}(\Delta)
satisfies the fixed point equation
\psi(\lambda^{*}(\Delta);\Delta)=\varphi(\lambda^{*}(\Delta))
which in turn can be written more explicitly as
Equation

I.4
.
∎

Remark I.1
.

Equation

I.4
is often used with
\Delta=\delta_{i}
(
i\in\{1,2\}
) under the condition
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
.
This is legitimate since
\overline{\lambda}(\delta)>\overline{\lambda}(\delta_{i})
(see, e.g.,
Equation

5.33
) and the latter condition is stronger than the one in
Lemma

I.2
.

Lemmas

I.3
and
I.4
below follow by using the definitions of various functions and parameters defined at the beginning of
Section

3
.
We omit the proofs.

Lemma I.3

(Equivalent characterization of spectral threshold)
.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, the following are equivalent.

1.

\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
;

2.

\psi(\overline{\lambda}(\delta);\delta_{i})<\varphi(\overline{\lambda}(\delta))
;

3.

\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i});\delta_{i})>\zeta(\overline{\lambda}(\delta);\delta_{i})
;

4.

\psi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i});\delta)>0
.

Lemma I.4

(Alternative formulas of overlaps, spectral)
.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, if
\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})>\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
, then

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}\cdot\frac{\frac{1}{\delta}-\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})-Z}\right)^{2}\right]}{\frac{1}{\delta_{i}}+\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z}{\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})-Z}\right)^{2}(G^{2}-1)\right]}}\,

\displaystyle=\,\sqrt{\frac{\psi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i});\delta)}{\alpha(\psi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i});\delta_{i})-\varphi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta_{i})))}}\,.

Theorem I.5

(
[
MM19
, Lemma 2]
)
.

Let
x^{*}\sim\unif({\mathbb S}^{d-1})
, and

A\in{\mathbb R}^{n\times d}
be a design matrix with i.i.d.
{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
entries.
Let
{\mathcal{T}}\colon{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}
be a preprocessing function and
T=\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{T}}(q(Ax^{*},{\underline{\varepsilon}})))
, where
q\colon{\mathbb R}\times{\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}
is a link function and
{\underline{\varepsilon}}\sim P_{\varepsilon}^{\otimes n}
is a noise sequence.
Suppose
n/d\to\delta
for a constant
\delta>0
.
Define random variables
(G,\varepsilon)\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\otimes P_{\varepsilon}
,
Y=q(G,\varepsilon)
and
Z={\mathcal{T}}(Y)
.
Assume
\Pr\left[Z=0\right]<1
and
Z
has bounded support.

Define functions
\varphi\colon(\sup\supp(Z),\infty)\to{\mathbb R}
and
\psi\colon(\sup\supp(Z),\infty)\times(0,\infty)\to{\mathbb R}
as

\displaystyle\varphi(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\lambda\,\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{ZG^{2}}{\lambda-Z}\right],\quad\psi(\lambda;\delta)=\lambda\left(\frac{1}{\delta}+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{\lambda-Z}\right]\right),

respectively.
Define
\overline{\lambda}(\delta)
and
\zeta\colon(\sup\supp(Z),\infty)\times(0,\infty)\to{\mathbb R}
as

\displaystyle\overline{\lambda}(\delta)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\lambda>\sup\supp(Z)}\psi(\lambda;\delta),\quad\zeta(\lambda;\delta)=\psi(\max\{\lambda,\overline{\lambda}(\delta)\};\delta).

Consider the matrix
D=\frac{1}{n}A^{\top}TA\in{\mathbb R}^{d\times d}
, and let

\lambda_{1}(D)\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{d}(D)
and
v_{1}(D),\cdots,v_{d}(D)
denote the eigenvalues and the corresponding eigenvectors.
Then, the following results hold:

1.

The equation
\zeta(\lambda;\delta)=\varphi(\lambda)
has a unique solution in
\lambda\in(\sup\supp(Z),\infty)
which is denoted by
\lambda^{*}(\delta)
.

2.

We have that, almost surely,

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\left|\left\langle v_{1}(D),x^{*}\right\rangle\right|}{\left\|v_{1}(D)\right\|_{2}\left\|x^{*}\right\|_{2}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\sqrt{\frac{\psi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta);\delta)}{\psi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta);\delta)-\varphi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta))}},&\psi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta);\delta)>0,\\
0,&\psi^{\prime}(\lambda^{*}(\delta);\delta)\leq 0.\end{cases}

3.

We have, almost surely,

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{1}(D)

\displaystyle=\zeta(\lambda^{*}(\delta);\delta),\quad\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{2}(D)=\zeta(\overline{\lambda}(\delta);\delta).

Remark I.2
.

The above theorem is a generalization of
[
LL20
, Theorem 1]
which additionally requires that the preprocessing function only takes non-negative values.

Theorem I.6

(
[
MM19
, Lemma 3]
)
.

Suppose that the following conditions hold:

1.

A\in{\mathbb C}^{n\times d}
is a random matrix whose entries are i.i.d. such that
\mathbb{E}\left[A_{i,j}\right]=0
,
\mathbb{E}\left[|A_{i,j}|^{2}\right]=1
and
\mathbb{E}\left[|A_{i,j}|^{4}\right]<\infty
;

2.

The empirical spectral distribution of
T\in{\mathbb C}^{n\times n}
converges weakly to a probability measure
\mu
as
n\to\infty
;

3.

n/d\to\delta\in(0,\infty)
as
n,d\to\infty
;

4.

The spectral norm of
T
is bounded for every
n\in{\mathbb Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Then, the empirical spectral distribution of
\frac{1}{d}A^{\top}TA
converges weakly to a measure whose inverse Stieltjes transform is given by

\displaystyle s^{-1}(z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{z}+\delta\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Z}{1+zZ}\right],\qquad z\in{\mathbb H}.

Remark I.3
.

The above theorem was first proved in
[
MP67
]
(see also
[
BS10
, Chapter 4]
) under an additional assumption that
T\in{\mathbb C}^{n\times n}
is PSD for all
n\in{\mathbb Z}_{\geq 0}
.
It was recently showed in
[
MM19
]
that the same conclusion holds without the PSD condition on
T
.
For a general diagonal
T\in{\mathbb R}^{n\times n}
(not necessarily PSD), the idea in
[
MM19
]
is to decompose
T
into
T_{+}
, consisting of only non-negative entries, and
-T_{-}
, consisting of only negative entries, and then apply free probability tools to study how they jointly contribute to the matrix product
\frac{1}{d}A^{\top}TA
.

Lemma I.7

(Lemma 4.5 in
[
DSS11
]
)
.

Let
(Q_{t})_{t\geq 1}
be a sequence of distributions converging weakly to some distribution
Q
, and let
h
be a non-negative continuous function such that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\int h\,{\rm d}Q_{t}=\int h\,{\rm d}Q.

(I.5)

Then, for any continuous function
\Psi
such that
\left|\Psi\right|/(1+h)
is bounded,

\lim_{t\to\infty}\int\Psi\,{\rm d}Q_{t}=\int\Psi\,{\rm d}Q.

(I.6)
